Storskogen · STOR.B-SE · the new Memo conveyor's card of 22 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote in the layers behind the Storskogen memo

These are the Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions it worked through before the Stock-specific Investment Case A&J Memo on Storskogen was written: 48,559 words, against 11,896 words in the memo Analyst's draft.

Word for word. Every answer below is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written. Two things are changed, both layout only: each group's judgement sits above the questions it sums up, because it was written last; and each score is shown as a pill. A script checked that every word of every answer is on this page, in order.

The Investment Analyst's terms: it calls Layer 1 "the first set", Layer 2 "the second set", and Layer 3 and Layer 4 "the third and fourth sets". "The Consumer" is the Investment Analyst who works the next layer. "Our" means our own forecasts, as opposed to the sell-side's. Acronyms are explained under "Terms used" at the end, and on a computer by hovering over a dotted term.

On request: a clarity-edited version, with a summary of the most important messages and a comparison with the memo, as made for Xvivo Perfusion on 30 October 2026.

How to read the scores

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning below its title.

The letter beside each score is the system's own grade of that score (A from 90, B from 75, C from 50, D from 25, F below 25). ❌ marks a D, ❌❌ an F.

Layer 1

Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

82 questions in 10 groups · 23,429 words · written 22 October 2026, 18:04 to 18:24 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Storskogen (STOR.B-SE): first set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Written 2026-10-22, the day before the Q3 2026 report (23 October 2026). Money in SEK unless stated. Where the sources disagree on a figure I say so and give both.

Orientation for the Consumer (read before the answers)

Storskogen is a Swedish serial acquirer of small and medium-sized businesses: 113 to 114 business units in three business areas, Industry (SEK 14,319m of 2025 sales, 43%), Trade (SEK 9,588m, 29%) and Services (SEK 9,232m, 28%), net sales SEK 33,097m in 2025, adjusted EBITA SEK 3,117m (9.4% margin, flat on 2024, 9.0% in 2023). The case has three layers that the Consumer should model separately, because they move on different clocks: (1) an organic cycle, dominated by Industry (industrial production niches are more than half the group) with a 20% to 25% construction and installation exposure inside Services and a Nordic consumer exposure inside Trade; (2) a financial repair that is now largely complete (interest-bearing net debt cut by about SEK 4.0bn to 4.6bn over three years to 2.3x to 2.4x adjusted EBITDA; net financial items falling from SEK 297m in Q2 2025 to SEK 146m in Q2 2026; bond and bank maturities pushed to late 2027, 2028, 2029 and 2030); and (3) a restarted acquisition engine, small so far (nine or so deals in 2026 to date, each SEK 90m to SEK 130m of sales, at or below 7x EBITA), which the company says will "step up quarter by quarter". The market prices the stock at 8.6x to 9.3x forward earnings and roughly 9x to 10x EV/EBITA, a 30% to 50% discount to proven Nordic compounders, with two visible brokers (DNB Carnegie Buy, target SEK 15.0; Nordea Buy, target SEK 16.0) whose 2026 EPS forecasts differ by 30% (SEK 1.11 against SEK 0.77). The single most important modelling fact: consensus "adjusted EPS" (SEK 1.03 median for 2026) is struck before amortisation of acquired intangibles (about SEK 700m a year, SEK 175m in Q2 2026 alone), while reported EPS was SEK 0.63 in 2025 against the brokers'-basis SEK 0.953. Model both.

External change forces

C5010 questions
C-0152C-0245C-0356C-0445C-0544C-0658C-0740C-0855C-0948C-1050

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The external picture for Storskogen is a split one, and the split runs along the same line as its portfolio: the larger, more international, industrial half faces a genuine but short-dated cyclical upturn (purchasing managers' indices at multi-year highs, order books rising, automation and electrification spending), while the Swedish construction and consumer-facing quarter of the group faces demand that has not turned and a central bank that is about to hike. No external force is structural in either direction; the forces are cyclical, uneven, and visible only a few months ahead. The macro force that matters most is the rate regime, which hits this company three times (its floating debt, its sector's multiple and its weakest end-market), and it is the reason that an otherwise improving backdrop should be modelled conservatively. For the operating model: assume Industry organic growth of mid-single digits through mid-2027 fading thereafter, Services flat to low single digits with margin recovery dependent on Swedish construction that has no two-year visibility, Trade flat with a krona-driven margin improvement, and reported growth 1 to 3 points below organic plus acquired growth on currency. For the financial model: carry the interest savings as banked but stress a 50bp to 100bp rise in floating cost.

Horizon: the last 6 months and the next 6 months, against the last 12 to 18 months.

C-01

End-market demand: structural or cyclical, and with what visibility

C52

Question asked To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

Judgement: demand is improving cyclically in the Industry business area (the larger half of the group), still falling or flat in Nordic construction (Services) and Nordic consumer retail (Trade), and the improvement has no two-year visibility; it is a short-cycle restocking turn with order books of months, not years. Treat it as cyclical, early, and uneven; do not model it as a structural change.

The evidence that the Industry turn is real: organic sales growth in Industry of 4% in Q1 2026 and 6% in Q2 2026 with "broad-based demand across all verticals, a particularly strong sales finish in June" and order books that "grew stronger heading into the second quarter"; the Swedish manufacturing purchasing managers' index at 58.1 and the Eurozone index at 52.9 in September 2026 (highest since May 2022); the Assistant Portfolio Manager's report that the European short-cycle destock ended in the first half of 2026; Indutrade's 8% organic order growth and 105% book-to-bill in Q2 2026. Against that, the two weaker legs have not turned: Services profitability "continued to be weighed down by weak underlying demand in Infrastructure Services exposed to the construction sector" with "intense industry-wide price pressure"; the Eurozone construction purchasing managers' index was 42.8 in June 2026, Swedish housing starts about 8,000 against 18,000 in 2021, and European swap rates rose 70bp in August and September 2026, delaying residential starts; Trade sales fell 1% in Q2 as Scandinavian retailers optimised inventory in health and beauty, and management flagged "customer restraint" for the seasonally weak Q3. The Product Solutions vertical inside Industry (larger factories with indirect consumer and construction exposure) runs at low capacity utilisation, so even Industry is split.

Visibility is the critical judgement for the model: the group's own earnings review describes Industry order books as "solid" but Services and Trade as "hostage to immediate seasonal shifts", and the sector precedent (Lifco) says backlogs run three to six months. Nothing here qualifies as a cyclical swing with two-plus years of visibility. The next six months bring one new negative to weigh against the industrial tailwind: a likely Riksbank hike in November 2026 into one of the most rate-sensitive economies in the developed world (mortgages reset quickly), which bears directly on the 45% of sales in Sweden and on the construction leg. Net: the demand backdrop is better than the last 12 to 18 months (organic growth was negative 6% in Q1 2024, 0% in Q4 2024, 2% in 2025), but the improvement is narrow and fragile.

C-02

Competitive intensity: direction and extent

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

Judgement: in the operating businesses, competitive intensity is unchanged except in construction-exposed Services, where it has risen as volumes fell (price competition in a shrinking pool); in the market that matters most to a serial acquirer, the market for acquiring small and medium-sized companies, intensity has risen modestly and structurally over three years, but head-to-head clashes with the proven compounders remain rare, so the practical effect on Storskogen is on the quality and size of what it can buy at 7x rather than on whether it can buy at all.

Operating markets: subsidiaries occupy "leading positions in specialized regional niches" and the group has 114 units averaging SEK 290m of sales, so competitive change is unit-specific and invisible at group level, with one exception: construction-exposed Infrastructure Services, where "private construction weakness has led to intense industry-wide price pressure" (DNB Carnegie) and the Services margin fell to 10.3% in Q2 2026 from 10.8%, and to 7.7% in Q1. Chinese low-cost imports are a localised threat in some product niches (the Sdiptech Rolec precedent), not yet documented at Storskogen.

Acquisition market: new entrants (Tången buying at an average 4.8x EV/EBITA, Auroora Group with 1,500 Swedish and 600 Baltic targets, Röko, InstallatørGruppen at 5.0x to 5.5x), continued activity from Indutrade (nine deals, SEK 1.5bn of sales in H1 2026), Addtech and Lifco expanding into continental Europe and the UK, and the Redeye warning that the growing number of public and private serial acquirers may push entry multiples up. Against that, the former Lifco head of acquisitions says 60% to 70% of deals see some competition but mostly from private equity, search funds and local buyers that are "easy to outperform" when the seller prioritises continuity, and that Lifco met Indutrade head-to-head only twice in eight years. Nordic compounders still pay 6x to 8x. Storskogen's own prospectus concedes that rivals with lower cost of capital can offer terms it cannot match. The direction is therefore mildly negative, and the mechanism is adverse selection: a rigid 7x cap in a crowding market buys smaller or lower-quality businesses, which is exactly how the 2021 to 2022 vintage went wrong (then by paying up to 10x).

C-03

Disruption force: strengthening or disproved, and direction

C56

Question asked To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

Judgement: no disruption force is being disproved; the forces at work (industrial automation, electrification, digitalisation of healthcare administration and of small-business workflows) are mild, long-dated tailwinds for the Industry area and for two or three recent acquisitions, and a mild execution burden for the Services area; net direction positive, magnitude small over the next 6 months, larger over three years.

Industrial Technologies and Automation project companies "benefited from past growth investments and strong global electrification trends" (Q2 2026 interim); the European reshoring trend favours automation suppliers that let local factories offset higher production costs; the Detab Ecomat SEK 100m-plus paperboard automation project and the PV System internal automation deployments are concrete instances. LEP (Swiss hospital documentation software, about 80% recurring revenue, acquired August 2025) and Duratec (German laboratory distributor with high recurring revenue, October 2026) extend the group into cycle-resistant digital niches. The burden side: the Services area must standardise and digitalise to become "efficient and scalable" (CEO, Q1 2026 call), which requires upfront spending across 100-plus autonomous units; group-wide AI training was rolled out in 2025 and 2026, and ERP rollouts are under way in Trade and Industry. Nordic SMEs digitalise faster than continental peers (Nexi), so the bar rises. None of this changes the next-six-month numbers; it changes the mix of what gets bought and the long-run margin of Industry.

C-04

Political or regulatory change and its bearing

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

Judgement: regulatory change is a modest net cost over the next six months (sustainability reporting, foreign-direct-investment screening on acquisitions, steel safeguards raising input costs, US tariffs on Swedish exports) and a modest tailwind over three years (climate-proofing of infrastructure supporting the 20% to 25% construction and installation exposure); it does not bear on the investment case's main variables.

The first CSRD statement was published on 9 April 2026 and "substantially increased administrative burdens", although the board applied phase-in reliefs and the EU Omnibus package may narrow scope (Jefferies warns that national gold-plating in the Nordics and DACH may prevent real relief). The Swedish Screening of Foreign Direct Investments Act, with fines up to SEK 100m, adds transaction complexity to the restarted acquisition programme. The EU steel safeguard from 1 July 2026 halves tariff-free quotas and doubles out-of-quota duties to 50%, with "melt and pour" traceability from 1 October 2026: steel is 32% of the group's Scope 3 emissions, so this is a real input-cost and compliance item for Industry. A temporary 10% US tariff on eight European nations including Sweden from February 2026 bears on export-oriented units. On the positive side, DNB Carnegie expects climate-proofing regulation to lift installation demand over the decade, and ISO and compliance capacity can help subsidiaries win larger B2B contracts against unlisted competitors. Net: a small negative for 2026 to 2027 margins (tens of millions of SEK, within the noise of a SEK 3.1bn EBITA base), and a small positive to the long-run Services demand case.

C-05

Input costs, availability and supplier concentration: headwind or tailwind

❌ D44

Question asked How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

Judgement: a headwind that is fading. Over the last six months input costs rose faster than sales (raw materials and consumables up 8% to SEK 4,884m in Q2 2026 against 5% sales growth, 55.1% of sales against 53.5%), wages rose in labour-intensive Services, fuel and freight rose on Red Sea rerouting, and precautionary early purchases tied up working capital; over the next six months the stronger krona turns into a procurement tailwind for Trade (purchases in euros and dollars, sales in kronor) with a one-to-two-quarter lag as old inventory clears. Supplier concentration is not a group-level issue.

The mechanism the Consumer should carry: Storskogen is a price-taker with "zero backward integration" and 114 fragmented purchasing points; central frame agreements (DHL, PostNord, Vattenfall saving SEK 20m a year, a central freight framework) offset a small share. Industry passed through raw-material inflation and lifted its margin to 11.0% from 10.4% in Q2 2026; Services could not (margin 10.3% from 10.8%, "higher material and fuel costs" plus price competition). The currency side is the swing factor: the discontinuation of currency hedges means Trade's import margins now follow the krona directly; in Q1 2026 transaction revaluations drove about 5 percentage points of the organic EBITA decline, and management expects a tailwind for Trade margins from Q2 2026 as inventory hedges expire. The latest reading (Q2) was broadly neutral on translation. Carbon pricing exposure (60% of Scope 3 emissions) is a long-dated cost. Availability is adequate; the risk is cost and the lag in passing it on under longer contracts (prospectus warning).

C-06

Where the company's own revenue and demand cycle sits, and is it turning

C58

Question asked Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

Judgement: the company's own cycle has turned up from a trough on the sales line and is at the inflection on the profit line: organic sales growth went from negative 6% in Q1 2024 to 0% in Q4 2024, about 2% for 2025, 2% in Q1 2026 and 5% in Q2 2026, and Q2 2026 was the first quarter of adjusted EBITA growth since Q4 2024; but organic EBITA was still negative 4% in the first half of 2026 (negative 12% in Q1) and the twelve-month adjusted EBITA margin (9.2% at September 2026 per DNB Carnegie) sits below both 2024 and 2025 (9.4%). Sales are turning; profit has not yet confirmed it.

Two cautions on the data. First, the sources disagree on 2025 organic growth: the company's annual report and the business-foundations work say about 2% organic in 2025 (translation cut reported growth by 2%), while DNB Carnegie's table shows negative 4% for 2025 organic sales growth and negative 3% in one review; the Consumer should rebuild the organic series from the quarterly reports rather than inherit either. Second, the sector research warns that recovery prints across this sector are "price, divestment, mix or working-capital release rather than demand"; at Storskogen, the risk report calls organic sales growth non-diagnostic because price inflation masks volume. The honest reading is that volume is recovering in Industry (order books, June finish), is flat in Services (Q1 projects delayed by frost were to shift into Q2, and Q2 Services sales grew 9% but mostly from the Safi acquisition, with 3% organic for the half), and is still down in consumer-facing Trade. The company's trailing cycle has lagged the Nordic peer set (Lifco organic 4.7% in Q2 2026, Sdiptech 11%, Indutrade 5%), which is consistent with its heavier Swedish construction and consumer weighting. The turn is real, early and not yet profitable.

C-07

Macro force: where it bites hardest

❌ D40

Question asked To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

Judgement: the dominant macro force is the interest-rate regime, and it bites in three places at once, hardest at the company level: (1) directly through floating-rate debt and a 2027 refinancing (company); (2) through the valuation of the whole compounder sector, whose multiples track the earnings-yield gap to swap rates (sector); (3) through Nordic construction and Swedish consumer demand (industry). The next six months carry a specific negative skew: a likely Riksbank hike in November 2026, the ECB priced for about 72bp of further tightening through September 2027, the US 10-year near 5.1%, and European swap rates up 70bp in two months.

Company level: net interest expense was SEK 828m in 2025 (SEK 1,044m in 2024) and the run rate has fallen toward SEK 600m a year after the September 2025 bond refinancing, but about SEK 10bn of net debt is floating (STIBOR-based) with SEK 3,899m of swaps of which SEK 2,000m expired within a year of end-2025; a 100bp move is of the order of SEK 60m to SEK 100m, 5% to 7% of 2026 pre-tax profit. Sector level: in the 2022 sell-off the Swedish compounders' median multiple halved from 30x to 15x cash operating profit (Redeye), and the Geography digest states the gap between these groups' earnings yield and the ten-year euro swap rate narrowed in Q3 2026. Industry level: 45% of sales in Sweden, 20% to 25% in construction and installation. The offsets are genuine: Swedish growth is strong (hence the hike), the Swedish manufacturing index is 58.1, and higher rates for longer "constrain competition" for cash-generative buyers. But the asymmetry is clear: a rates shock hurts Storskogen's financing, its multiple and its weakest segment simultaneously, which is why the portfolio's Fragility tilt is Double Underweight and Sweden was moved from Overweight to Neutral this month.

C-08

Industry- or sector-level driver accelerating the company's demand

C55

Question asked To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

Judgement: yes, one driver is accelerating demand in the larger half of the group: the European short-cycle industrial turn (destock ended in H1 2026, purchasing managers' indices above 50 and rising, automation and electrification capital spending) is lifting Industry's order intake and organic growth to 5% to 6%; it does not reach Services or Trade, and at group level it is worth perhaps 2 to 3 percentage points of organic growth (DNB Carnegie's estimate of a 4 percentage point boost from Industry applies to the segment's share) rather than a step-change.

What makes it credible: the breadth of the evidence (Sweden 58.1, Germany 53.9, Eurozone 52.9; Addtech's beat-and-raise with sales up 27%; Indutrade's orders; the sector cohort of "newly lit acquirers" that the Assistant Portfolio Manager expects to win the six-to-eighteen-month window). What limits it: Storskogen's Industry is a mix of project-driven automation companies (benefiting) and volume factories in Product Solutions (not), and management noted that Q2's strong June finish was partly project timing, which the earnings review flags as a possible reversal in later quarters. The question for the Consumer is whether this driver persists into 2027; the sector reading is "watch pre-buying and a restock plateau by the fourth quarter". Model it as 2026 to mid-2027 support, not as a multi-year trend.

C-09

Other material external dynamics

❌ D48

Question asked What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

Judgement: three other dynamics are material. (1) The krona: Storskogen is unhedged by policy (hedges discontinued for 2026), 46% of operating profit and 55% of sales are outside Sweden, translation reduced 2025 sales growth by 2% and Q1 2026 by 3% before turning neutral in Q2; a stronger krona is a reported-growth headwind and a Trade margin tailwind at the same time, and the Nordea path (EUR/SEK 10.50 by end-2026) implies continued drag on reported growth. (2) The sector narrative: the market this month favours "newly lit or repaired acquisition engines whose earnings can outrun a de-rating" (Storskogen named first), and consensus "models only businesses already owned", so each acquisition is an unpriced upgrade for 12 to 24 months; this is a positive dynamic specific to Storskogen's position. (3) Sector contagion: the January 2026 short-seller note on Lifco that brokers could not obtain, and Volati's collapse (negative 76% over twelve months), keep the cohort's risk premium high and make any Storskogen stumble read as sector confirmation.

The CHF point deserves a line: the Swiss franc is 22% of group net assets on 4% of sales (a 1% CHF move is SEK 44m of translation reserve), so equity and therefore the covenant-relevant equity ratio (50% at end-2025) move with a currency the business barely trades in. The krona dynamic is the most important of the three for the 18-month model because it determines whether the organic turn shows up in reported SEK.

C-10

Each thematic driver in turn, then all together: headwinds and tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level

C50

Question asked Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

Judgement by level. Value chain (customers, suppliers, intermediaries): tailwind in industrial automation and electrification customers; headwind in Nordic construction customers and Nordic retail intermediaries; input costs a fading headwind turning to a krona-driven tailwind in Trade. Net slightly negative now, neutral by mid-2027. Industry (industrials and capital goods): tailwind, the short-cycle turn with purchasing managers' indices at multi-year highs; the Assistant Portfolio Manager's hypothesis is a six-to-eighteen-month window favouring cheap, geared laggards and newly lit acquirers. Net positive. Sector (diversified industrial groups and serial acquirers): headwind from the discount rate (compounder multiples hostage to swap rates, markets refusing re-ratings, Fragility Double Underweight), tailwind from the "repaired balance sheet buying again" pattern that produces unpriced upgrades. Net neutral to slightly negative on the multiple, positive on earnings revisions.

Overall external change picture: mildly positive for earnings over the next six to twelve months (Industry turn, interest savings already banked, krona procurement tailwind), negative for the discount rate and for the weakest segment (Riksbank hike, construction), and low in visibility. The external tailwinds are strong enough to make the sell-side's flat-margin 2026 forecasts look achievable, not strong enough to carry the company to its own 10% margin and 15% EBITA-growth targets without internal action.

Internal change forces

C5213 questions
C-1145C-1262C-1338C-1456C-1562C-1650C-1745C-1858C-1955C-2055C-8640C-8748C-2154

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Storskogen's internal change picture is that of a disciplined repair that has succeeded and a growth restart that has only just begun. The hard evidence of change is financial: net debt down about SEK 4bn to 4.6bn in three years, leverage from 4.35x at the 2022 peak to 2.3x to 2.4x, net financial items down by half, nine loss-making units and two consumer-retail chains sold, parent costs halved, incentive hurdles rewritten around EBITA growth and cash conversion. The soft evidence of change is operating and is weaker: the Services area has had three leaders in a year and its interim leader is also running the acquisition restart; organic EBITA is still negative; there is no internal audit as the unit count begins to rise again; and the acquisitions so far are too small to move a SEK 33bn group. The strategy is clear and sensibly cautious (7x cap, themes, B2B, non-Swedish), and it is funded by cash the company now has, but it is a strategy to restore the model, not to widen a moat, and its mid-term targets (15% EBITA growth, 10% margin) are retained rather than raised and were missed in 2025. The Consumer should model the internal changes as a reliable step-down in financing cost and a modest, back-end-loaded acquired growth contribution, and should hold the Services margin recovery and the integration quality of new deals as the two internal variables on which the 18-month forecast most depends.

Horizon: the last 6 months and the next 6 months, against the last 12 to 18 months.

C-11

Changes in chief executive, key leaders, board and key actors: extent and signal

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

Judgement: the top is stable and the middle is not. Christer Hansson (permanent CEO since July 2024, internal, ex-head of Trade, ex-Dustin) and CFO Lena Glader (since 2019) are unchanged and their mandate (cash, deleveraging, discipline) has been delivered; the board was refreshed in May 2026 with Adam Parker (founder of Trivariate Research, ex-Eminence Capital and Morgan Stanley) replacing Robert Belkic (ex-CFO of Hexagon), which adds an international investor's perspective and removes a financial-controls veteran from the audit committee; but the Services business area, the weakest segment, has had three heads in under twelve months (co-founder Peter Ahlgren to senior advisor; Jesper Kronstrand appointed 1 February 2026 and resigned 18 May 2026 "for personal reasons"; Head of M&A Johan Ekström interim since), so the person responsible for the M&A restart is also running the turnaround of the segment most in need of one.

What the changes signal. The CEO and CFO continuity signals that the repair agenda is institutionalised: the 2026 Share Savings Programme vests fully only at 15% annual adjusted EBITA growth with a 7.5% floor and also tests cash conversion, which binds management to the targets it has so far missed. The Parker appointment signals a board that wants a capital-markets view on allocation now that growth resumes. The Services churn signals that the company could not retain an operator it had publicly backed, in the segment where the margin gap to the 10% target is largest; DNB Carnegie's standing warning is that an organisation built for fast growth may fail to retain talent when ambitions are moderated, and the 54% rate of change among business-unit chief executives and 17% group employee turnover in 2025 say the same at subsidiary level. Co-founders Alexander Bjärgård (chair of the investment committee, senior advisor, "dependent" director) and Ahlgren remain influential through 56% to 64% of the votes. Net: moderately negative on execution capacity for the next six months, neutral on direction.

C-12

Change in strategy or strategic priorities: how well articulated and resourced

C62

Question asked To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

Judgement: a clear and well-articulated pivot, from balance-sheet repair ("Phase 3", 2023 to 2025) to growth (from late 2025), with specific choices: five investment themes (automation, digitalisation, energy and sustainability, health and wellbeing, infrastructure); platform acquisitions at 7x EBITA or below targeting EBITA of SEK 20m to SEK 50m; a shift toward B2B and away from consumer retail; a priority for non-Swedish and less cyclical niches (Sweden below 45% of sales); verticals cut from 14 to 7; and a stated cadence ("the pace of capital allocated to acquisitions will step up quarter by quarter"). It is resourced by cash flow rather than debt: with debt reduction near zero in 2026, free cash flow of roughly SEK 1.5bn to SEK 2.1bn a year (consensus) plus about SEK 0.5bn of headroom to the CFO's 2.5x ceiling funds SEK 1.5bn to SEK 2.5bn of acquisitions a year.

The strength of the articulation is also its limit: this is a repair-and-redeploy strategy, not one that widens a moat. The themes are the same as every Nordic compounder's, the 7x discipline is reactive to the 2021 to 2022 mistakes (up to 10x paid, 119 deals in two years, SEK 920m impairment in 2024), and the operational excellence agenda for Services (standardisation, digitalisation, ERP) is necessary housekeeping. Resourcing of the organisation to execute both the acquisition restart and the Services turnaround is the weak point: the M&A head is the interim Services head, headquarters has 79 people (against Lifco's 16 and Indutrade's 24) but the sector research notes Storskogen was left with "insufficient central investment managers" after rapid growth. The strategy is credible as written; its execution bandwidth is the open question.

C-13

Guidance raised, mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions set or raised

❌ D38

Question asked To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

Judgement: nothing has been raised. Storskogen gives no annual guidance ("I don't want to guide", Hansson, Q2 2025), and its mid-term targets are unchanged from the November 2024 Capital Markets Day: average annual adjusted EBITA growth of 15% for 2025 to 2027, adjusted EBITA margin above 10% over a rolling three-year cycle, organic growth above GDP plus 1 to 2 points, cash conversion above 70%, leverage 2.0x to 3.0x, dividend 15% of net earnings. The 2025 outturn missed two of them (EBITA growth negative 3%, margin 9.4%). The only new quantified ambition in the last six months is the Share Savings Programme hurdle (15% full vesting, 7.5% floor through 2029), which restates the target rather than raising it.

The Consumer should read the retention of the 15% growth target through 2027 as a stretch that both covering brokers already discount (DNB Carnegie: "highly challenging"; consensus EBITDA growth 0.7% in 2026 and 5.4% in 2027). The CEO has implicitly softened it ("I continue to believe that our target of at least 10% is reasonable, especially if the economic environment improves"), which is closer to a conditional reaffirmation than a raise. There is no scheduled capital-markets day in the files. Net: no positive change force from guidance.

C-14

Standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline

C56

Question asked To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

Judgement: yes, in a measurable and credible way on cost and capital discipline, less so on controls and on operating standards in the weakest segment. The choices and actions: cash conversion held above target (104% in 2023, 97% in 2024, 74% in 2025 and 74% on a rolling basis at June 2026, against 70%); net working capital cut from 17.5% of sales in Q3 2022 to about 15% by Q4 2023 (FactSet's broader definition shows 18% to 19% of sales outside year-end); parent administrative expenses down to SEK 167m in 2025 from SEK 307m; about 1,000 full-time equivalents cut in Services; capital expenditure held at 1.6% of sales in 2025 (rising to 1.9% to 2.3% in 2026 for growth investment in Industry); standardised COSO key controls in smaller subsidiaries raised from 24 to 37; ERP rollouts in Trade and Industry; a leadership framework programme and AI training across units; and incentive hurdles now tied to EBITA growth and cash conversion.

What has not improved: there is still no internal audit function across 113 to 114 units in about 30 countries (the Tungdykargruppen SEK 30m charge of 2020 is the precedent; the sector research says subsidiary failures in this model surface late through impairments and fraud); quarterly cash conversion fell to 48% in Q2 2026 from 66% a year earlier as receivables built SEK 378m, which management calls timing; and the Services area, where "operational excellence" is the declared top priority, delivered a 7.7% margin in Q1 and 10.3% in Q2. The pattern is improving financial hygiene at the centre and uneven operating standards at the edge, which is the characteristic shape of a decentralised group one cycle after over-expansion.

C-15

Reducing the perimeter: divestments, simplification, exits from low-return units

C62

Question asked To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

Judgement: a real and completed reduction, now reversing into addition. Actions: eleven divestments in 2023; nine low-performing units divested in 2024 with a SEK 920m non-cash impairment, removing SEK 1,512m of low-margin sales and SEK 110m of losses; Motavo (hair salons, SEK 280m of sales, margin below 10%) sold in late 2025; Perfect Hair (Swiss online beauty, SEK 278m of sales, adjusted EBITA negative SEK 19m in 2025) sold on 2 March 2026 at a SEK 65m capital loss; verticals cut from 14 to 7 and internal mergers of units; business units down from about 124 to 113. DNB Carnegie's credit team credits the clean-up with lifting the adjusted EBITA margin; the change-forces work estimates the divestments add about 40 basis points.

Two implications for the model. First, the perimeter reduction has already delivered most of its margin benefit (the 2026 base is clean of Motavo and Perfect Hair), so there is little further mix uplift from exits unless management prunes sub-10% Services units, which it has not announced. Second, each exit so far has come with a loss (SEK 920m in 2024, SEK 65m in Q1 2026), so the reported-to-adjusted gap is not only amortisation; the Consumer should carry a modest recurring "divestment and restructuring" charge below adjusted EBITA rather than treat these as finished.

C-16

Entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly

C50

Question asked To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

Judgement: the company is entering new geographies steadily and mostly sensibly through bolt-on-sized platforms inside existing verticals, but the footprint is widening faster than the centre's capacity to oversee it. 2025 to 2026 entries: Switzerland (LEP, healthcare documentation software, August 2025), the UK (Darlington EMS, electronics manufacturing for automation, April 2026; Chris Pullen made permanent UK managing director in February 2026, the UK being the "strongest pipeline"), Singapore (Safi Equipment & Services, fire safety, May 2026, SEK 106m of sales), Norway (Verdant AS, professional haircare distribution, July 2026, NOK 133m), Germany (Duratec, laboratory distribution, October 2026, about SEK 112m), Benelux (Hawedo, protective equipment, October 2026, SEK 93m, via Båstadgruppen as an export hub). Finland (Frameda, beauty distribution) and UK (SGS Tool Group) are earlier.

Sensible: each sits inside a named theme and vertical, most bring recurring or regulation-driven revenue (Safi, LEP, Duratec), the haircare moves (Verdant) rebuild a professional B2B platform where the consumer-facing units were sold, and non-Swedish exposure reduces the Riksbank and construction concentration. Less sensible: Singapore is a long way from a Stockholm head office with no internal audit; the group already runs about 400 legal entities in 30 countries; and the deals are small (SEK 90m to SEK 130m of sales each against SEK 33bn), so the geographic diversification is "largely aspirational" (earnings review) at the group level. The product side is a narrowing, not an entry: consumer retail out, B2B distribution and recurring services in.

C-17

Raising prices in real terms: real, nominal, or latent

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

Judgement: nominal, not real, and segment-dependent. Industry demonstrated cost-recovery pricing in Q2 2026 ("successfully implemented strategic price increases" to offset raw materials; margin 11.0% from 10.4%); management claims that aggressive price adjustments protected group sales despite industrial volumes falling 20% from 2022 highs; and Q2 2026 group margins were flat underlying, which management reads as pricing sufficient to offset wage inflation. But Services faces "intense industry-wide price pressure" in construction-exposed units and could not pass through material and fuel costs; Trade's import margins moved with the krona because pricing lagged currency by two quarters; raw materials rose to 55.1% of sales from 53.5%; and DNB Carnegie's own reading is "weak pricing power: intense competition and high material costs limit Storskogen's ability to pass price increases to customers". The risk report notes the lack of organic EBITA by vertical makes true pricing power unassessable from outside.

The judgement for the model: assume price recovers cost in Industry with a one-quarter lag, recovers cost in Trade with a two-quarter lag (and currently benefits from the krona), and does not fully recover cost in construction Services until volumes return. There is no evidence of real price rises above cost inflation anywhere in the group, and management has not claimed any; what management increasingly notes is that AI tools are being deployed for "local pricing strategies", which is latent at best.

C-18

Stepping up mergers and acquisitions: strategic sense, discipline, integration

C58

Question asked To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

Judgement: a genuine step-up from a near-freeze, disciplined on price as far as can be seen, strategically coherent, and as yet small and unproven on integration. Pace: 54 deals in 2022, 12 in 2023, essentially none in 2024, nine in 2025, and in 2026 three platforms and three add-ons in the first half (DNB Carnegie) plus Verdant in July, Duratec and Hawedo in October, with management saying the pace will "step up quarter by quarter" and that international markets are nearly half of the pipeline. Discipline: all at 7x EBITA or below per management and DNB Carnegie, against the 2021 to 2022 record of up to 10x; the target platform size (EBITA SEK 20m to SEK 50m) is deliberately small. Strategic sense: themes, B2B, recurring revenue, non-Nordic; several are add-ons to existing platforms (Hawedo to Båstadgruppen, Verdant into haircare), which is lower-risk than new platforms.

The scale point is the one the Consumer must hold: SEK 867m of acquisition spend in 2025 and SEK 204m in the first half of 2026 (FactSet) against SEK 33bn of sales and a SEK 27.6bn enterprise value; DNB Carnegie models a 5% annual sales contribution from M&A, ramping in 2027 and 2028, and credits 5% a year to EPS. To matter, the cadence must rise several-fold from here, and the sector precedent (Sdiptech targeting SEK 100m of acquired EBITA a year; Lifco adding SEK 0.6bn of EBITA in 2025) shows what a working engine does. Integration evidence is the thinnest part: goodwill was 78% of purchase price in the 2026 combinations, minority put obligations rose to SEK 1,740m at mid-2026 from SEK 1,473m, and the Services head running M&A limits the integration bandwidth. Peers' lesson (Lifco: no earn-outs, sellers keep equity; three years of operating history required) is a benchmark Storskogen has moved toward but not stated.

C-19

More use of the balance sheet, or stepping up shareholder returns

C55

Question asked To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

Judgement: modestly more use, directed at acquisitions rather than returns. Leverage sits at 2.3x to 2.4x within a 2.0x to 3.0x target, with the CFO wanting to stay below 2.5x, so the headroom actually being used is about 0.1x to 0.2x of EBITDA (SEK 0.4bn to SEK 0.8bn); debt reduction, which took SEK 900m in 2025 and the majority of free cash flow in 2024 to 2025, is "close to zero" in 2026, which is the real change: roughly SEK 1.5bn to SEK 2bn of annual cash now goes to deals instead of lenders. Shareholder returns: a SEK 100m buyback (8,865,000 B shares) completed in January 2026, the dividend raised to SEK 0.11 from SEK 0.10 (a 15% payout policy, SEK 0.13 to SEK 0.16 on consensus), and the share savings programme settled in cash to avoid dilution. Management has not mooted larger returns; the explicit priority is acquired growth.

The Consumer should treat the balance sheet as a constraint that binds at 2.5x: with EBITDA of about SEK 4.1bn to SEK 4.4bn, every 0.1x of leverage is about SEK 0.4bn of capacity, and the 2027 bond and bank maturities plus minority buyout obligations (SEK 1,740m of put options; earn-outs winding down) compete for the same cash. The buyback is a signal, not a capital-allocation lever (0.5% of the market capitalisation).

C-20

Other material positive corporate change forces present or plausible

C55

Question asked What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

Judgement: four are present and one is plausible. Present: (1) the interest saving from the September 2025 refinancing of the 2027 bond into a 2029 bond at a considerably lower rate (SEK 20m a quarter, about SEK 80m a year, worth about 5% of EPS), with net financial items falling to SEK 146m in Q2 2026 from SEK 297m; (2) the wind-down of earn-out and minority-buyout commitments from the 2021 to 2022 wave, which management says are "approaching their structural end", freeing cash flow; (3) the tax line, where internal restructuring enabled more efficient interest deductions (reported tax rate 24.4% in 2025 against 28.5% in 2023 and a distorted 76.5% in 2024); (4) credit: S&P's outlook moved to stable from negative, bank facilities extended to 2028 and 2029, no maturities in 2026. Plausible: a credit-rating upgrade (Moody's B1 from March 2023; S&P BB) as leverage stabilises, which would cut the cost of the late-2027 refinancing.

Two of these (interest and earn-outs) are one-time step-downs that flatter 2026 to 2027 EPS growth and then stop, which is why the 2027 consensus EPS growth of 21% should not be extrapolated; the Consumer should model them as a bridge, not a trend.

C-86

Negative corporate change developments present or plausible

❌ D40

Question asked What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

Judgement: present: (1) the Services leadership vacuum and the double role of the M&A head; (2) a cash-conversion downtrend (rolling 74% from 79% in Q1 and 88% a year earlier; quarterly 48%) just as growth investment (capital expenditure 2.3% of sales in Q2) and acquisitions resume; (3) organic EBITA still negative (negative 4% in H1 2026); (4) the discontinuation of currency hedging, which exposes Trade margins and reported EBITA to the krona with no derivative buffer; (5) the absence of an internal audit function as the unit count starts to rise again; (6) a chief executive with a "relatively minor" direct shareholding and no open-market insider purchases. Plausible: a Riksbank hike-driven extension of the Swedish construction slump into 2027, which would hold Services below target and make the 15% EBITA growth target unreachable; a rise in acquisition multiples that pushes the 7x discipline into lower-quality targets; and a repeat of impairments or earn-out reversals at acquired units (the risk report's FALSE FRIEND profile).

The one development that would most change the case is the first: if a permanent Services head is not appointed and the segment margin does not recover toward 10% by mid-2027, the margin target fails regardless of Industry.

C-87

Controlling actors indicating scope for change in strategy or approach

❌ D48

Question asked To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

Judgement: none indicated. The three founding partners (Bjärgård, Bergström, Ahlgren) hold Class A shares with ten votes each, 7.4% of capital and 44.5% of votes, and with associated holdings 56% to 64% of the votes; Bjärgård chairs the investment committee and Ahlgren is a senior advisor, so the controllers are inside the tent and back the current strategy. The nomination committee received no shareholder proposals for the 2026 AGM; there is no activist, and the only outsider added is Adam Parker, whose Trivariate Research background is analytical rather than activist. The founders' conversion of 7,000,000 Class A shares into Class B in December 2025 improves liquidity at the margin and could be read as a step toward a simpler structure, but no one has said so.

The implication is stability of direction and an absence of external pressure to accelerate value creation (no buyback pressure, no break-up pressure); the dual-class structure also removes the take-out floor that some sector names enjoy.

C-21

Taken together: strength, breadth and resourcing of the positive corporate changes

C54

Question asked Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

Judgement: the positive internal changes are real, broad across the financial dimensions (leverage, interest cost, cash discipline, perimeter, incentives) and narrow across the operating dimensions (one segment is still broken and its leadership is interim); they are resourced by cash flow rather than debt, which is the right way round; and they are small in scale relative to the group (deals of SEK 100m of sales each into a SEK 33bn group; a SEK 100m buyback). The honest characterisation is a company that has finished fixing its balance sheet and is in the first two or three quarters of redeploying, with the operational fix of its weakest third still ahead.

For the model, the positive internal changes justify: a step-down in net financial items to about SEK 600m a year; acquired sales growth of 2% to 4% in 2026 rising to 4% to 6% in 2027 to 2028 if the cadence holds; a modest margin uplift from mix and divestments; and an organisation that will struggle to run both the restart and the Services turnaround at once, which argues for assuming the Services margin reaches 10% later than management hopes.

Industry and Value Chain

❌ D4816 questions
C-2445C-2548C-2646C-2735C-2855C-2955C-3148C-8455C-3248C-3348C-3448C-3546C-3655C-3747C-3850C-3947

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The industry that matters for Storskogen's economics is the Nordic and European market for buying founder-owned small companies, and that industry is structurally kind to the business model (an inexhaustible pool of succession-driven sellers, stable 6x to 8x pricing, large competitors that behave rationally and rarely bid against each other) and structurally unkind to Storskogen's position in it (bottom of the quality ranking, higher cost of capital, a compounding spread of two to three turns against twelve to fifteen for the leaders, and a reputation with sellers dented by the 2024 divestments). The empirical record of the sector says that companies in Storskogen's current position, balance sheet repaired and buying again at disciplined prices, earn a 12 to 24 month window of unpriced upgrades, and that the window closes when leverage nears its ceiling, when organic growth fails to confirm, or when rates re-rate the whole cohort. The operating markets add two concentrated exposures, Nordic construction (20% to 25% of sales, still falling, price-competitive) and Nordic consumer retail (weak, destocking), and one broad tailwind, the industrial short-cycle turn in the larger half of the group. The Consumer should build the model with the acquisition engine as an explicit, dated line (deal count, size, multiple, cost of funding), the Industry area as a cyclical that has turned, Services as a cyclical that has not, and the sector multiple as a function of rates that the company cannot influence.

A framing note: a serial acquirer competes in two markets, the market for buying companies and the hundred-odd operating markets of its subsidiaries. For Storskogen the first is where the group-level economics are made or lost; the second is diffuse (114 units, average SEK 290m of sales) and matters mainly through its two concentrated exposures, Nordic construction (20% to 25% of sales) and Nordic consumer retail (inside Trade). The answers below treat the acquisition market as the "industry" unless stated.

C-24

Rank by market share or size, and distance from the number one or two position

❌ D45

Question asked Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

Judgement: by size Storskogen is one of the two or three largest Nordic serial acquirers (SEK 33.1bn of 2025 sales against Lifco SEK 28.3bn, Addtech SEK 22.7bn, Indutrade of a similar order, Sdiptech SEK 5.2bn, Volati SEK 4.3bn post-Salix), but size is not rank in this industry; rank is set by return on capital, margin and cost of capital, and on those Storskogen is at the bottom of the proven peer set (EBITA margin 9.4% against Lifco 22.4%, Addtech 16%, Indutrade 13% to 14%; ROCE about 10% company-defined and 6.8% on DNB Carnegie's basis against Lifco 20.5% including goodwill and 132% excluding it). In the acquisition market no participant has meaningful share: the top five Nordic compounders control less than 5% of the regional SME market and the European pool is about 12 million SMEs with about 25,000 quality targets. At subsidiary level the units are claimed to hold "leading positions in specialized regional niches", which cannot be verified from outside and is contradicted in construction Services by price-taking behaviour.

The distance to number one or two is therefore a distance in quality, and it is large: the 30% to 50% valuation discount to Lifco, Addtech and Indutrade is the market's measure of it. Storskogen is also the youngest of the large names (founded 2012, listed 2021) with the shortest cycle-tested record.

C-25

Market concentration: top five above 30 percent, and steepness of the distribution

❌ D48

Question asked How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

Judgement: the acquisition market is extremely unconcentrated (top five below 5%), so there is no concentration-derived pricing power for buyers; the operating markets are hundreds of local niches, individually concentrated (a top-three local position is the usual acquisition criterion) but collectively invisible at group level. The one group-level operating exposure where structure matters, Nordic construction and installation services, is fragmented and competitive, with price falling when volume falls.

The implication for the Consumer is that industry structure gives Storskogen neither protection nor threat at group level; its economics are a function of what it pays, how it integrates and how its two cyclical exposures behave. The fragmentation of the target pool is the sector's long runway (DNB Carnegie: established compounders can maintain their historical pace through 2031), and the fragmentation of the operating niches is why the group's margin is a blended average that no single competitor can attack.

C-26

Stability of market shares and new entrants' share over three to five years

❌ D46

Question asked How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

Judgement: in the acquisition market, shares have shifted toward new and specialised entrants over three to five years (Tången at 4.8x average entry multiple, Auroora, Röko, InstallatørGruppen, Momentum Group at 24% acquired growth, search funds, private equity in the EUR 1.5m to 2.5m EBIT bracket) and away from Storskogen itself, which went from the most active Nordic acquirer (119 deals in 2021 to 2022, 63 over 2022 to 2025) to near-zero in 2024; the proven compounders (Lifco 16 to 17 deals a year, Indutrade nine in H1 2026) kept their pace. In the operating markets, share stability is the norm in the niches and share loss is the risk in construction Services and consumer Trade (the units that were sold were losing share and money).

The point for the model is that Storskogen's "share" of Nordic deal flow is now set by its own capacity and discipline, not by the market, and that new entrants have raised the floor on competition for the smallest, cheapest targets, which is the bracket Storskogen's 7x cap steers it toward.

C-27

Consolidation toward the largest and most advantaged competitors, and is this company among them

❌ D35

Question asked To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

Judgement: yes, this industry consolidates toward the advantaged, and the advantage is not size but cost of capital, reputation with sellers and integration capability; value and deal flow accrue to the buyers who can self-fund at 1x to 2x leverage, pay 6x to 8x, and are known by founders as permanent, hands-off owners (Lifco, Indutrade, Addtech, Lagercrantz; Indus in Germany at 5.5x to 6.5x EV/EBIT on the same reputational basis). Storskogen is not among the advantaged today: its cost of capital is higher (bonds at STIBOR plus 265bp against Lifco at STIBOR plus 100bp; equity at 9x against 20x-plus), its reputation with sellers was damaged by the 2024 divestments (a seller to Storskogen now knows the owner may sell), and its integration record carries a SEK 920m impairment and a documented fraud. It is, however, moving back toward the advantaged set on leverage and discipline, and it is "the purest deal-restart case" in the cohort (Assistant Portfolio Manager, 13 October 2026).

The mechanical consequence: the compounding spread (multiple paid against multiple at which the parent trades) that drives this industry is about 2x to 3x turns for Storskogen (7x paid, 9x to 10x parent) against 12x to 15x for Lifco (7x paid, 20x-plus parent), so each krona deployed re-rates far less; this is why the model's upside from M&A is modest unless the parent multiple expands.

C-28

Competitors' attitude to competition: margin or volume first, benign or loss-tolerant, do prices fall when volume falls

C55

Question asked What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement: the Nordic compounders put margin and return on capital first (Lifco tells managers to ignore market share and maximise absolute profit; Sdiptech would "rather say no to deals"; Indus spent only half its M&A budget rather than overpay), they are benign toward each other in deals (rare head-to-head bidding), and they do not suffer losses to win; this is a rational, price-disciplined set at the group level. In the operating niches the attitude varies and is unobservable at scale; in Storskogen's one concentrated exposure, construction-exposed Services, competitors are volume-first in a downturn and industry prices fall when volume falls, which is why Services margins compressed with flat sales.

The relevant precedent for Storskogen's own behaviour is that it was itself the shark in 2021 to 2022, paying up to 10x and "buying everything that breathed" (former Lagercrantz vice president), which inflated the market and damaged its returns; it now behaves like the disciplined set. Peer discipline is a modest positive for the industry's economics and a modest constraint on Storskogen's growth (the easy deals are the ones the disciplined names decline).

C-29

Stability of that attitude over eighteen months to three years; O'Leary instability and shark-attack risk

C55

Question asked How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement: the compounders' attitude is stable and predictable; none shows O'Leary-style reactivity and none has launched a price attack on the others in three years; the instability in the industry came from the levered newcomers (Storskogen itself in 2021 to 2022; Volati's post-Salix leverage) and from the market's valuation of them, not from competitive behaviour. The risk of a shark attack on acquisition pricing is low: the disciplined names refuse to overpay, and the levered names cannot. In construction Services, contractor behaviour is reactive and price-led in downturns, which is the one place where competitors would "attack" on sensing weakness, and the weakness is now.

The one change to watch over the next three years is the proliferation of small public and private acquirers; if credit loosens, a new cohort could repeat Storskogen's 2021 behaviour and inflate entry multiples again. Over the next eighteen months, with rates rising, that is unlikely; higher-for-longer rates "constrain competition" for cash-generative buyers (DNB Carnegie).

C-31

How supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support

❌ D48

Question asked Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

Judgement: supportive for the business model's growth runway (a near-unlimited pool of succession-driven sellers, stable 6x to 8x entry multiples, rational large competitors, fragmented operating niches that protect blended margins), unsupportive for Storskogen's position within it (bottom of the quality ranking, higher cost of capital, a smaller compounding spread), and fragile in two places: the rate regime that sets the whole sector's multiple and therefore its compounding spread (halved in 2022), and the Nordic construction cycle that sets the profitability of a quarter of the group. The support from structure is therefore conditional on rates and on Storskogen's own behaviour rather than inherent.

C-84

Historical precedents, rules of thumb and empirical evidence on how companies in this sector outperform and underperform, and the company's fit

C55

Question asked What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

Judgement: the empirical pattern for Nordic serial acquirers is clear and the company's fit with it is the whole investment debate.

Outperformance pattern: (1) self-funded acquisitions at 6x to 8x EBITA adding 8% to 12% a year to EBITA, on top of mid-single-digit organic growth, for a decade or more (Lifco 18% EBITA CAGR 2015 to 2025, 12 points from M&A Indutrade 15% EBITA CAGR 2013 to 2023 at 14% median ROIC; Lagercrantz 23% median ROE); (2) leverage held at 1x to 2x so the engine runs through downturns; (3) high returns on tangible capital (Lifco ROCE 132% ex-goodwill; Sdiptech core units above 60%) with capex at 1% to 3% of sales; (4) margin-first culture and a tiny centre (Lifco 16 head-office staff, Indutrade 24); (5) honest, guidance-free communication matched by delivery. The market pays 20x to 26x EV/EBITA for this pattern, and the sector insight is that "a serial acquirer's multiple is the market's guess at how long the engine runs, and organic growth is its evidence". Underperformance pattern: debt-funded over-expansion into a rate rise (Storskogen 2021 to 2023, Volati 2026), paying 9x to 10x, impairments and earn-out reversals, organic growth stalling while acquired growth masks it (the "false friend" reading at Lifco, Indus and Storskogen alike in 2026), and construction or consumer concentration (Volati's S:t Eriks; Storskogen's Services). The market applies a 30% to 50% discount to that pattern and holds it until a full cycle of delivery.

The recovery pattern, which is the one that applies now: "three of four serial acquirers in a reset have repaired their balance sheets and are buying again, and because consensus models only businesses already owned, each acquisition is an unpriced upgrade, a window of roughly 12 to 24 months before leverage nears its ceiling again" (Assistant Portfolio Manager, 13 October 2026); and in a short-cycle turn "the cheap, geared names with costs cut and the newly lit acquirers win". Sdiptech is the cleanest recent precedent (ten divestments, leverage 3.31x to 2.81x, organic growth 11%, shares up 42% over twelve months); Indus (up 69%) is the reset plus a commodity windfall; Volati (down 76%) is the control case where leverage stayed high.

Storskogen's fit: it fits the underperformance pattern historically (119 deals in two years, up to 10x, 4.35x leverage at peak, SEK 920m impairment, ROCE 7% to 10%, margin 9.4%, head office of 79) and the recovery pattern currently (leverage 2.3x to 2.4x, deals restarting at 7x, interest falling, divestments done, shares up 17% over twelve months and 9% over three). What it does not yet fit is the outperformance pattern: organic growth has only just turned positive, organic EBITA is negative, ROCE is a third of the leaders', and the compounding spread is narrow. The empirical lesson is that the recovery window pays for 12 to 24 months if deals arrive and organic growth confirms; the Consumer should model the engine's contribution explicitly (deal count, average size, multiple, timing) rather than assume peer-like compounding.

C-32

Customer demand: support for the thesis, and fragility

❌ D48

Question asked How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

Judgement: customer demand supports the thesis in Industry (order books, automation, electrification, 5% to 6% organic growth, margin up to 11.0%), does not support it in Services (construction customers postponing projects, price pressure, margin 7.7% to 10.3%) or in consumer Trade (retailers destocking, health and beauty weak), and is moderately supportive in professional B2B Trade (Professional Products "solid", protective equipment stable). Fragility is high: backlogs are months long; the Industry strength has a project-timing component; Q3 is seasonally weak with "customer restraint" flagged; and the Swedish consumer and construction exposures face a rate hike. Demand supports a 2026 stabilisation, not yet the 15% growth or 10% margin targets.

C-33

Route to market and intermediaries: helping or hindering, and fragility

❌ D48

Question asked To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

Judgement: mostly direct and neutral, with one hindrance. Industry and Services sell business-to-business, largely direct, so intermediaries are not a factor. Trade's distribution units sell through Scandinavian retailers, and those intermediaries are hindering now (retailers "optimised their working capital and inventory levels", depressing underlying demand in health and beauty) while professional channels help (salons, laboratories, industrial safety buyers); management's response is to shift Trade toward B2B distributors with proprietary brands (Verdant's exclusive KEVIN.MURPHY and Davines rights, Duratec's laboratory relationships, Hawedo's sourcing). The position is fragile where it depends on exclusive brand rights (which can be withdrawn) and on retailer inventory cycles (which reverse); a restocking cycle in Nordic retail would flip the hindrance into a tailwind within two quarters.

C-34

Existing competitors' behaviour: help or hurt, and the fragility of the company's position

❌ D48

Question asked How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

Judgement: at group level the existing competitors (the proven compounders) help by behaving rationally in the deal market and by setting a valuation ceiling that Storskogen can grow toward; they hurt by taking the best targets on better terms and by widening the quality gap every year (Addtech 13% sales growth with pricing power; Indutrade nine deals in a half). In operating markets, competitors hurt in construction Services (price competition) and in some product niches (low-cost imports), and are benign elsewhere. Storskogen's position against the proven names is fragile in the sense that it has nothing they lack except a lower price; its position against the newcomers (Tången, Auroora) is stronger on scale, pipeline and bank relationships.

C-35

New entrants: adjacent, standard, orthogonal; threat of each type

❌ D46

Question asked To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

Judgement: in the acquisition market, standard entrants (new listed and private compounders: Tången, Auroora, Röko, InstallatørGruppen; search funds) are a medium threat to deal yields in the smallest bracket; adjacent entrants (private equity moving down-market, family offices) are a low-to-medium threat; orthogonal entrants (AI-enabled deal sourcing, automated matchmaking) are a low threat today. In the operating markets, standard entrants are a low threat in the niches, Chinese low-cost hardware is a medium threat in any commoditisable product line (the Sdiptech Rolec precedent: 60-plus suppliers, hardware at USD 25 to 40), and orthogonal digital entrants threaten services units that have not digitalised (the Sampo and Nexi evidence on SME channel shift).

C-36

Substitutes or alternatives: threat and change

C55

Question asked To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

Judgement: low and stable at group level because the portfolio is 114 unrelated niches; the only group-level substitution dynamics are (1) customers of Trade distributors buying direct or online (which is why consumer retail was sold), (2) automation substituting for labour in Services (which Storskogen is itself selling into through Industry), and (3) digital tools substituting for manual services processes. None is accelerating in a way that is visible in the group numbers; the risk is unit-specific and surfaces as an impairment rather than as a trend.

C-37

Regulators and political actors: help or hurt, and fragility

❌ D47

Question asked What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

Judgement: a small net hurt in 2026 to 2027 (CSRD administrative cost, FDI screening on every Swedish acquisition with fines up to SEK 100m, steel safeguards raising input costs, carbon pricing exposure on 60% of Scope 3 emissions, US tariffs on Swedish exports, workplace accidents up to 83 in 2024 from 66) and a small long-run help (climate-proofing and electrification regulation supporting installation and infrastructure demand; ISO and compliance capacity as a selling point to larger B2B customers). Nothing political targets the company or the sector; the fragility is in the Swedish rate and housing policy mix, which is a central-bank matter rather than a regulatory one, and in the possibility that EU Omnibus relief is gold-plated away nationally.

C-38

On whom in the value chain disruption forces act, how, and the fragility they leave

C50

Question asked On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

Judgement: on customers, disruption acts positively (industrial customers automating and electrifying, hospitals digitalising, fleets and buildings being retrofitted), and Storskogen is a supplier into those changes through Industry and through LEP and Safi; on competitors, disruption acts through the proliferation of acquirers and through low-cost imports, both negative for Storskogen's returns; on regulators, disruption acts through sustainability and traceability mandates that raise fixed compliance cost, which a decentralised group bears less efficiently than a centralised one; on suppliers, disruption acts through shipping-route and trade-barrier shocks (Red Sea, steel safeguards, tariffs) that a price-taker with no backward integration absorbs with a lag. The fragility these leave is the pass-through lag: Storskogen's subsidiaries can usually reprice within one or two quarters, but in fixed-price project contracts (Services) and in currency-exposed imports (Trade) the lag is longer and the margin is lost in the meantime.

C-39

Suppliers: support or risk

❌ D47

Question asked Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

Judgement: a modest source of risk, not of support. The supplier base is fragmented across 114 units with "a fragmented, non-coordinated supplier base exposed to steel and wood price volatility and Suez Canal-driven shipping disruption"; Trade buys in euros and dollars against krona sales without hedges; the group's own risk assessment places its most serious supply risks in high-risk countries; and raw materials rose to 55.1% of sales in Q2 2026. The supports are small: central freight and energy frame agreements (SEK 20m of savings from Vattenfall), the Storskogen Knowledge Exchange for procurement, subsidiaries evaluating alternative supplier locations, and precautionary inventory that secured availability into Q2 at the cost of working capital. The krona's strength is the one supplier-side tailwind for the next two to three quarters.
