Company - Competitive advantage

❌ D407 questions
C-4135C-4240C-4365C-4442C-4542C-4635C-4740

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Storskogen's competitive advantage is the weakest foundation in the case, and the Consumer should build the model as if the group had no moat and a collection of locally positioned but undifferentiated subsidiaries. The group's genuine assets are scale in sourcing and financing relative to new entrants, an unlimited addressable acquisition market, and a professionalised ownership platform; its genuine deficits are the absence of proprietary intellectual property or purchasing power, price-taking in its construction-exposed quarter, a head office that is a diseconomy rather than a lean allocator, and a compounding spread of two to three multiple turns against twelve to fifteen for the leaders. The only evidence of pricing power is cost pass-through in Industry, and the only mechanical forces working in the company's favour are financial step-downs that run for 18 to 24 months and then stop. This means the operating model should assume margins converge slowly toward 10% through mix and self-help rather than through pricing or scale, that returns on new capital are set by the price paid (about 11% to 14% EBITA yield at 7x, before integration costs) rather than by any advantage, and that the gap to the proven compounders does not close on its own. The investment case therefore rests on execution and the cycle, not on the quality of the business.

C-41

Sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats: which, how strong, widening or narrowing

❌ D35

Question asked Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

Judgement: Storskogen has no group-level moat; it has a collection of subsidiary-level local advantages (customer relationships, regional niche positions, in a few cases proprietary brands or recurring software) and one group-level capability that is a partial moat for a serial acquirer, the reputation and process for buying founder-owned companies. Assessed by type: customer-related, moderate at the unit level and unverifiable at the group level, narrowing in construction Services; production-related, weak, no proprietary IP, no backward integration, "independent price-takers"; scale-based, weak (procurement savings of SEK 20m on SEK 33bn of sales); regulatory or political, weak except where units hold certifications (Safi's fire safety, LEP's hospital standard); culture or knowledge, moderate and narrowing (decentralised entrepreneurship preserved, but 54% of unit chief executives changed, 17% turnover, and a centralised 79-person head office that the sector's best run with 16 to 24). The business-foundations work rates confidence in advantaged business strength as LOW and says the model "lacks proprietary IP, subsidiary-level pricing power, and a capital reinvestment engine capable of consistently generating the 25 to 30 percent post-tax RONIC required".

Net: the group's advantage over an unlisted competitor is access to capital and a professionalised owner; its advantage over a listed compounder is none. The moat, such as it is, is neither widening nor narrowing at group level; it is being rebuilt at the margin by buying units with recurring revenue (LEP about 80% recurring, Duratec, Safi).

C-42

Scale relative to competitors and other actors, and what it confers

❌ D40

Question asked How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

Judgement: large in sales (SEK 33bn; the largest or second largest Nordic compounder), small in everything scale is supposed to confer. The 114 units average SEK 290m of sales and operate as "siloed companies that prevent synergy extraction"; scale confers group-wide frame agreements (freight, energy, logistics) worth tens of millions, a bank syndicate and bond market access that smaller compounders lack, a UK and international deal pipeline, and a brand with sellers; it does not confer purchasing power (zero backward integration), lower unit costs, or cheaper capital (bonds at STIBOR plus 265bp against Lifco's plus 100bp). Scale has also conferred a diseconomy: a central organisation of 79 (a "headquarters staffing premium of 230% to 395%" over Lifco and Indutrade) and about 400 legal entities across 30 countries without an internal audit function. Against the newcomers (Tången, Auroora, Sdiptech at SEK 5bn) scale is a real advantage in sourcing and financing; against the leaders it is not.

C-43

Realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and mid-term growth allowed

C65

Question asked How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

Judgement: the addressable acquisition market is effectively unlimited relative to the company (about 12 million European SMEs, about 25,000 that pass quality filters, 626,000 German businesses alone needing succession by 2027, the top five Nordic compounders at under 5% share); it allows acquired growth of 5% to 10% a year for a decade, as the proven peers demonstrate, and the binding constraints are capital (SEK 1.5bn to SEK 2.5bn a year at current leverage), organisational capacity (the number of deals a 79-person centre with an interim Services head can diligence and integrate), and price discipline, not the market. The addressable operating markets of the subsidiaries allow organic growth of GDP plus 1 to 2 points (the company's own target), which is what the diversified peers achieve over cycles (Lifco about 6%, Sdiptech about 6% ten-year average). Mid-term growth allowed: 8% to 12% a year in EBITA if the engine runs at the peers' cadence; 4% to 6% if it runs at the 2025 to 2026 cadence.

C-44

Surplus customer utility against competing offerings: leading, or demonstrably not inferior

❌ D42

Question asked How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

Judgement: cannot be shown to be leading, and inferiority can be partly disproved only in Industry. Industry's ability to raise prices with raw-material costs in Q2 2026 and expand margin to 11.0% while growing 6% organically is the one piece of evidence that its units' offerings are not inferior (a customer paying more and buying more); Professional Products in Trade showed "good overall demand for specialised distribution and proprietary brands". The disproof fails in construction Services (price pressure, margin compression on flat sales, a unit-level price-taker profile) and in the consumer units that were sold because they lost money (Motavo, Perfect Hair). At the group level, DNB Carnegie reads "weak pricing power" and the expert network reads "minimal operational synergies between highly diverse portfolio business units". The honest statement is that the surviving portfolio is probably "not inferior" in its niches, with the construction exposure the exception, and that nothing in the record suggests leadership.

C-45

Demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of surplus utility, shown by application

❌ D42

Question asked To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

Judgement: demonstrable only as cost pass-through in Industry, with a lag; not demonstrable as real pricing anywhere. The applications on record: Industry's Q2 2026 price increases offsetting raw materials with margin up 60 basis points; management's claim that price adjustments protected group sales through a 20% industrial volume decline from 2022; and the absence of "severe cost increases" in early 2026 that management could not handle. The counter-applications: Services could not pass through material and fuel inflation in Q2 2026 (margin down 50 basis points) and faces "intense price pressure"; Trade's margins followed the krona for two quarters because repricing lagged; raw materials rose to 55.1% of sales; and organic EBITA fell 4% in H1 2026 on 3% to 5% organic sales growth, which is the signature of price recovering cost, not exceeding it. The risk report's conclusion that organic sales growth is "non-diagnostic because price inflation masks volume contraction" cuts both ways: it means some of the reported organic growth is price, which proves the ability to pass through cost, and it means that volume is weaker than the headline, which proves the absence of surplus utility that would hold volume at higher prices.

C-46

Sharing scale economies with customers, stated or de facto

❌ D35

Question asked To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

Judgement: not a feature of the model, stated or de facto. The decentralised structure means scale economies (such as they are) are captured within clusters and kept; there is no group-wide pricing philosophy of passing savings to customers (the opposite, in fact: the serial-acquirer playbook, documented at Lifco, is to raise prices 3% to 7% after acquisition). Storskogen's messaging is about entrepreneurial autonomy and owner permanence, not customer economics. This is neutral for a niche B2B portfolio where customers buy reliability rather than price, and it is a structural reason the model has no flywheel.

C-47

Gravitational or mechanical forces improving competitive advantage and financial outputs against peers each period

❌ D40

Question asked To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

Judgement: the mechanical forces at work are financial and temporary, not competitive and cumulative. Mechanical and positive for the next 18 months: the interest step-down (SEK 80m a year banked), the earn-out and minority-buyout wind-down, the roll-off of divested losses, the krona procurement lag in Trade, and the arithmetic of buying at 7x inside a 9x to 10x vehicle (each SEK 1bn deployed at 7x adds about SEK 140m of EBITA, roughly 4.5% of the base, before financing). Mechanical and negative: amortisation of acquired intangibles rising with each deal (SEK 700m a year now), minority put liabilities rising (SEK 1,740m), a 15% dividend payout and a 2.5x leverage ceiling that cap reinvestment. Against peers, the gravitational force runs the other way: Lifco's engine adds 12% a year to EBITA at 132% tangible ROCE and a 20x-plus parent multiple, so each period the leaders compound further ahead whatever Storskogen does; the margin and return gap is widening in absolute terms even as Storskogen improves. There is no mechanism in Storskogen's model, such as a data advantage, a network effect or a cost curve, that improves its competitive position against peers by itself with time.

Company - Economic strength

❌ D4211 questions
C-4948C-5050C-5130C-5252C-5338C-5435C-7550C-7645C-7748C-7852C-7932

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Storskogen's economics are those of a mediocre business that has been made safe again: a 9.4% adjusted EBITA margin that has not moved in three years, returns on capital of 6.8% to 10.2% that are a third to a half of the proven peers', an earnings base in which a third of adjusted EPS is amortisation of the price paid for past deals and in which impairment and divestment charges recur, and leverage that is no longer dangerous (2.4x) but still amplifies every move in operating profit by roughly one and a half times. The strengths are real and should be modelled: cash conversion of 70% to 80% of adjusted EBITDA on a capital-light base (capex 2% of sales, 62% return on working capital), free cash flow of SEK 1.5bn to SEK 2.4bn a year, an interest line that has fallen by a quarter and will fall further, and an unlimited addressable market in which capital can be deployed at about 10% to 11% post-tax returns. What the economics do not offer is a reason for the margin or returns gap to the peers to close on its own: there is no pricing power beyond cost recovery, no scale economy, no group-wide cost programme, and the improvement path is many small, slow, congruent levers (Services recovery, Trade mix, pruning, procurement, working capital) that together take the margin from 9.4% toward 10% to 10.5% by 2028, which is where consensus already sits. The EPS growth that consensus shows (8% in 2026, 21% in 2027) is mostly financial step-downs that are banked and finite; the durable operating growth rate is 6% to 10% a year with the engine running at its current cadence. For the operating and financial model, the Consumer should: use both the adjusted and reported EPS bases; build the three business areas with their own drivers, seasonality and drop-through (high in Services and Product Solutions, low elsewhere); carry amortisation, minority puts and a recurring restructuring charge; and treat financial leverage as the amplifier that makes the cone of 2027 to 2028 outcomes wide in both directions.

A note on bases, because this group is where they bite. The financials block carries four EPS bases. Reported basic EPS was SEK 0.63 in 2025 (negative SEK 0.03 in 2024 after the SEK 920m impairment, SEK 0.47 in 2023). The brokers' adjusted basis, which excludes amortisation of acquired intangibles and items the company adjusts, was SEK 0.953 for 2025 (SEK 0.867 in 2024, SEK 0.767 in 2023), and consensus is on that basis (median SEK 1.03 for 2026, SEK 1.25 for 2027, SEK 1.22 for 2028; the 2028 median falls because the two brokers are at SEK 1.36 and SEK 1.22 and FactSet's median handling flips). Adjusted EBITA (company) was SEK 3,117m in 2025; FactSet's adjusted EBIT, after about SEK 700m of amortisation of acquired intangibles and other items, was SEK 2,048m (6.2% margin). The gap between the two EPS bases is roughly SEK 0.32 a share, a third of adjusted EPS, and it is persistent because every acquisition adds intangibles. The Consumer should carry both lines through the model and present valuation on both.

C-49

EPS growth being generated, with what revenue growth, and how durable

❌ D48

Question asked What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

Judgement: adjusted EPS growth of about 10% in 2025 (SEK 0.953 from SEK 0.867) and about 8% expected in 2026 (SEK 1.03 median; DNB Carnegie SEK 1.11, Nordea SEK 0.77), on revenue growth of negative 3% in 2025 and plus 3% to 4% in 2026 (SEK 34.2bn), so the EPS growth is almost entirely financial (interest and tax) rather than operating; 2027's consensus EPS growth of 21% (to SEK 1.25) on 5% revenue growth is the combination of the full-year interest saving, a modest margin recovery and the first meaningful acquired contribution, and it is the year the model will be judged on. Durability: the interest and tax components are durable at their new level but do not repeat; the organic component is cyclical; the acquired component is durable only if the cadence rises several-fold and integration holds. Underlying durable EPS growth, once the step-downs are banked, is in the 6% to 10% range (3% to 4% organic EBITA growth plus 3% to 5% from acquisitions at current cadence plus modest buybacks), not the 15% the company targets and not the 21% consensus shows for 2027.

The quarterly evidence: Q2 2026 adjusted diluted EPS SEK 0.23 from SEK 0.19 (up 21%) with adjusted EBITA up only 2%, net profit up 46% on net financial items halving; Q1 2026 adjusted EPS flat at SEK 0.13 with EBITA down 9%. This is EPS growth from the finance line, which is real cash but finite. Reported EPS tells a harsher story: SEK 0.63 for 2025 and SEK 0.09 plus SEK 0.21 for H1 2026, with divestment losses still recurring.

C-50

Immediate scope to deploy capital at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what it means for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple

C50

Question asked How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

Judgement: the scope is real but the return is moderate, not high. Capacity: free cash flow of SEK 1.5bn to SEK 2.4bn a year on consensus (2026 to 2028), less dividends of about SEK 0.2bn to SEK 0.27bn, less minority buyouts and residual earn-outs (unquantified; SEK 1,740m of put liabilities outstanding), plus leverage headroom of SEK 0.4bn to SEK 0.8bn to the 2.5x ceiling, gives SEK 1.2bn to SEK 2.5bn a year of deployable capital, about 4% to 9% of enterprise value a year. Return on new invested capital: at 7x EBITA the pre-tax EBITA yield is 14% and the post-tax return about 10% to 11% on the purchase price, before transaction costs, central overhead allocation, earn-outs and the integration risk that history says is material; with goodwill at 78% of purchase price, the return on tangible capital is far higher but the return on the cash spent is the one that matters. That is comfortably above the cost of debt (about 5% to 6%) and around the cost of equity, which makes the deployment value-accretive but not transformational.

What it means for the fair multiple: the deployment adds roughly 4% to 7% a year to EBITA at current cadence, which, with 3% to 4% organic growth, supports a low-double-digit EV/EBITA multiple in a normal rate regime (DNB Carnegie's 12x on 2026 at its SEK 15 target; 10x on 2027) against about 9x to 10x today; it does not support the 20x-plus of the peers, because those multiples price 25% to 30% post-tax returns on reinvestment, which this model has never produced (the business-foundations work puts the required RONIC at 25% to 30% and the company's record well below it). Mid-term potential: EBITA of SEK 3.6bn to SEK 4.0bn by 2028 is reachable (consensus EBITDA SEK 4.6bn); long-term potential depends on whether the parent multiple expands enough to widen the compounding spread.

C-51

Returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed, on tangible capital

❌ D30

Question asked How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

Judgement: low at group level, acceptable on tangible capital, and the gap between the two is the legacy of what was paid in 2021 to 2022. Return on capital employed: 10.2% in 2025 (company basis; 10.0% at Q1 and Q2 2026; 10.4% in 2024) and 6.8% on DNB Carnegie's basis against a proven-peer median of 16.6% and Lifco's 20.5% including goodwill. Adjusted return on equity 6.4% in 2025 (5.6% in 2024; 9.4% on another basis in the earnings review; 6.1% reported for the twelve months to June 2026). Return on working capital 62.2% (62.5% in 2024), capex 1.6% to 2.3% of sales, so the operating units earn high returns on tangible capital; capital employed is "bloated by legacy goodwill of SEK 18,124m" (43% of assets). Return on new invested capital: about 10% to 11% post-tax at 7x EBITA as derived in C-50, which is above the group average and therefore accretive to ROCE at the margin, but only slowly, because new deployment of SEK 1.5bn to SEK 2.5bn a year is 5% to 8% of capital employed.

For the model: ROCE rises toward 11% to 12% by 2028 if EBITA grows 5% to 8% a year and capital employed grows with deployment; it does not approach the peers' levels within the horizon. The returns gap is the fundamental reason for the valuation discount and will not close on this evidence.

C-52

Conversion of net income into free cash flow, and the behaviour of net working capital

C52

Question asked How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

Judgement: conversion is good on the company's measure and the FactSet measure, flattered in 2023 to 2024 by working-capital release, normalising now, and heading lower as growth and investment resume. Company adjusted cash conversion (operating cash flow to adjusted EBITDA after capex and leases, roughly): 104% in 2023, 97% in 2024, 74% in 2025, 79% rolling at Q1 2026, 74% at Q2 2026 (80% a year earlier), target above 70%; quarterly 48% in Q2 2026 (66% a year earlier) on a SEK 378m receivables build after a strong June. FactSet: operating cash flow SEK 3,361m in 2023, SEK 3,098m in 2024, SEK 2,451m in 2025; free cash flow after fixed-asset capex SEK 2,495m, SEK 2,269m, SEK 1,732m; free cash flow to reported net income 1.6x in 2025 (distorted upward by amortisation and the low net income base); consensus free cash flow SEK 1,541m in 2026, SEK 2,087m in 2027, SEK 2,410m in 2028 (operating cash flow SEK 2,829m, SEK 3,161m, SEK 3,236m; capex SEK 613m to SEK 656m).

Working capital: FactSet's receivables plus inventories less payables runs at 18% to 19% of trailing sales in the three quarters away from year-end and 22.7% to 23.4% at year-end (a seasonal and acquisition-timing pattern the Consumer should model quarterly); the company's narrower measure fell from 17.5% of sales in Q3 2022 to 15.0% by Q4 2023. Management has repeatedly said the 97% to 104% conversion of 2023 to 2024 was unrepeatable inventory and receivables liquidation. The structural reading: a 70% to 80% conversion of adjusted EBITDA is the right run-rate, free cash flow of SEK 1.5bn to SEK 2.0bn a year is the right base for deployment, and the Q2 2026 quarterly dip is likely timing (the earnings review's own next check), but the risk report's "false friend" warning that inventory liquidation masked margin pressure in 2023 to 2024 is a fair reading of the history. Earn-outs, minority buyouts (SEK 1,740m of puts) and lease payments sit below FactSet's free cash flow and reduce the cash available for deals.

C-53

Gross and operating margins, and their trend

❌ D38

Question asked How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

Judgement: gross margin 35% to 40% (DNB Carnegie's figure; FactSet's gross profit line breaks in 2025 to 6.3% because of a classification change and must not be used); raw materials and consumables 55.1% of sales in Q2 2026 (53.5% a year earlier). Adjusted EBITA margin 9.0% in 2023, 9.4% in 2024, 9.4% in 2025, 9.2% on a twelve-month basis at September 2026, 8.1% in Q1 2026, 9.7% in Q2 2026 (10.0% a year earlier, flat excluding a SEK 22m incentive reversal); by area in Q2 2026, Industry 11.0% (10.4%), Services 10.3% (10.8%; 7.7% in Q1), Trade about 9% (SEK 212m on SEK 2,315m). FactSet adjusted EBIT margin 5.5% in 2022 and 2023, 5.9% in 2024, 6.2% in 2025; consensus EBIT margin 7.0% in 2026, 7.7% in 2027, 8.2% in 2028, which implies adjusted EBITA margins of about 9.2% to 9.5%, 9.7% to 10.0% and 10.2% to 10.5% (adding back about SEK 700m to SEK 750m of amortisation). Trend: flat for three years at the group level, with mix (consumer exits) and Industry pricing pulling up and Services pulling down.

Against peers the gap is structural: Lifco 22.4%, Sdiptech core 21.5%, Addtech 16%, Indutrade 13% to 14%, Indus 8.5% to 9% normalised, Volati 7%. For the model: a 10% adjusted EBITA margin by 2027 to 2028 requires Services back above 10% for a full year and Trade above 9%; the quarterly seasonality (Q1 and Q3 weak, Q2 and Q4 strong, with Q1 margins in the 8% range) must be built in, and DNB Carnegie's sensitivity (0.5 percentage points of margin is SEK 165m of EBITA and about 11% of EPS) shows how much rests on it.

C-54

Cleanliness of the earnings base: adjusted against reported, one-offs, capitalised costs, tax and other leakage

❌ D35

Question asked How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

Judgement: the earnings base is materially less clean than the adjusted figures imply, in three layers. Layer 1, amortisation of acquired intangibles: about SEK 700m a year (SEK 175m in Q2 2026), excluded from adjusted EBITA and adjusted EPS; it is non-cash but it is the accounting cost of the acquisition engine, and it grows with every deal. Layer 2, recurring "non-recurring" items: the SEK 920m impairment of 2024, the SEK 65m capital loss on Perfect Hair in Q1 2026, a SEK 22m incentive-provision reversal that flattered Q2 2025 and Q3 2024, the SEK 38m of currency-hedge losses in Q1 2026, transaction currency losses of SEK 51m in operating profit and SEK 42m in financial items in 2025; items of this kind have appeared in most years. Layer 3, below-the-line: the reported tax rate was 24.4% in 2025, 28.5% in 2023 and 76.5% in 2024 (impairment non-deductible), and the 2025 improvement came partly from an internal restructuring that increased interest deductibility, which the change-forces work calls a non-operational boost that "cannot be sustained indefinitely without ongoing structural changes"; minority interests are small in the income statement (SEK 1m in FactSet's capital structure) but SEK 1,740m of minority put options sit as liabilities and are revalued; goodwill of SEK 18.1bn to SEK 18.5bn (43% of assets) has absorbed one SEK 920m write-down and remains exposed. Capitalised development costs are not material. The adjusted-to-reported EPS gap was SEK 0.953 against SEK 0.63 in 2025 (34%), SEK 0.867 against negative SEK 0.03 in 2024, SEK 0.767 against SEK 0.47 in 2023 (39%).

For the model: value on reported EPS as well as adjusted, carry amortisation as a permanent line rising with deployment, assume a recurring SEK 50m to SEK 100m of divestment and restructuring charges, hold the tax rate at 24% to 26% with a risk to 27% to 28% if the interest-deduction structure is challenged, and track minority put liabilities as debt-like.

C-75

Fixed-cost operating leverage: sensitivity of operating profit to revenue, and the range of outcomes it creates

C50

Question asked How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

Judgement: moderate at group level and asymmetric by segment. The arithmetic from DNB Carnegie: a 1% change in net sales moves adjusted EBITA by about SEK 31m (1% of EBITA, 2% of EPS), which is close to proportional and implies a largely variable cost base at the group level (materials at 55% of sales, decentralised labour that units flex locally, central costs only SEK 167m); a 2% volume change moves EBITA by up to SEK 100m (3%, with EPS 9% to 11%), which captures the incremental gross margin of 35% to 40% on volume as opposed to price. The segment evidence shows where the fixed costs sit: Services EBITA fell 30% in Q1 2026 on flat sales (frozen ground, start-up costs, under-utilised crews), and Product Solutions factories in Industry run below capacity, so a volume recovery in those two places would carry high drop-through (perhaps 25% to 35% incremental margin), while Trade distribution and project-based Industry units are close to variable.

Range of outcomes: on a SEK 33bn to SEK 36bn revenue base, plus or minus 3 points of organic volume moves adjusted EBITA by roughly SEK 150m to SEK 300m (5% to 10%) and adjusted EPS by 10% to 20% before financial leverage, which is wide enough that the 2027 consensus (EBITA about SEK 3.4bn to SEK 3.5bn) could be missed or beaten by 10% on volume alone. The Consumer should model Services and Product Solutions with 25% to 35% drop-through on volume and the rest of the group with 12% to 15%.

C-76

Financial leverage or gearing: amplification of EPS and risk, and comfort

❌ D45

Question asked How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

Judgement: moderate and tolerable, no longer threatening, and still the largest single amplifier in the case. Net interest-bearing debt about SEK 9.9bn at June 2026 (FactSet net debt SEK 9.9bn, 36% of enterprise value; company figure SEK 9,896m); interest-bearing net debt to adjusted EBITDA 2.4x on the company's basis, 2.65x on FactSet's basis including leases, against a 2.0x to 3.0x target, a CFO ambition below 2.5x and a 3.5x covenant; net interest expense SEK 828m in 2025 (SEK 1,044m in 2024, SEK 1,005m in 2023) and a run rate trending toward SEK 600m (consensus pre-tax profit implies about SEK 600m in 2026); EBITA interest cover about 3.8x in 2025 rising to 5x or more. Amplification: with net financial items at about SEK 600m against EBITA of about SEK 3.2bn, a 10% change in EBITA moves pre-tax profit by about 13% to 15%; a 100 basis point rise in STIBOR on the unhedged floating portion (net debt of about SEK 10bn less SEK 3.9bn of swaps, of which SEK 2.0bn expired within a year) costs SEK 60m to SEK 100m, 4% to 6% of 2026 pre-tax profit. Comfort: no maturities in 2026, bank facilities extended to 2028 and 2029, bonds in 2029 and 2030 (SEK 1bn 2025/2030 at STIBOR plus 265bp, down from 687.5bp at the 2023 peak), but a major bond and bank refinancing falls in late 2027, within the forecast horizon, and FFO to debt below 20% was the credit analysts' downgrade trigger; Moody's B1 and S&P BB remain sub-investment grade.

The judgement for the model: leverage amplifies the EPS recovery in 2026 to 2027 (the interest step-down is a large part of the growth) and would amplify a 2027 to 2028 downturn equally; a 15% EBITA shortfall (DNB Carnegie's bear) takes leverage to about 2.8x to 3.0x, which is survivable but ends the acquisition programme.

C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible

❌ D48

Question asked How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

Judgement: at the centre, little remains: parent administrative expenses were cut to SEK 167m in 2025 from SEK 307m and the head-office transfer to Storskogen Management AB was cost-neutral; a further SEK 50m to SEK 80m is conceivable by moving toward peer head-office sizes (79 people against 16 to 24), about 2% of EBITA. At the units, the cost base (materials 55% of sales, personnel perhaps 25%, other operating costs) is cuttable only unit by unit, which the model does well in a downturn (about 1,000 full-time equivalents cut in Services in 2023 to 2024; local managers "cut variable costs instantly" in this sector) and does not do centrally; there is no group cost programme and the decentralised structure precludes one. Reducible structurally: Services standardisation and digitalisation (unquantified, slow), ERP consolidation in Trade and Industry, procurement frame agreements (SEK 20m banked), and the exit of sub-10% units, which removes cost and revenue together. Interest cost (SEK 828m in 2025) is the one line that has been materially reduced and could fall further with a rating upgrade in 2027.

For the model: assume central costs flat, a modest Services productivity gain of 50 to 100 basis points over two years, and no group-wide cost programme; the cost-cutting lever is defensive (it protects margins in a downturn) rather than a source of upside.

C-78

Scope to improve financial outputs beyond cost-cutting over the mid term; peer precedents; few big or many small; congruence with strategy

C52

Question asked Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

Judgement: moderate scope from many small sources, with peer precedents for each, and all congruent with the stated strategy. The sources: (1) Services margin from 7.7% to 10.3% (quarterly range) to a sustained 10% to 11%, via construction recovery, standardisation and pruning (worth SEK 100m to SEK 150m); (2) Trade mix toward B2B and proprietary brands (Verdant, Duratec, Hawedo) and the krona procurement tailwind, worth 50 to 100 basis points on SEK 9.6bn (SEK 50m to SEK 100m); (3) exit of remaining sub-10% units (DNB Carnegie's 40 basis points estimate for past divestments suggests a further 20 to 40 basis points available); (4) net financial items from SEK 828m to about SEK 600m (banked) and lower again on a rating upgrade; (5) working capital from 19% toward 15% of sales (SEK 1bn-plus of cash, not profit); (6) tax efficiency; (7) acquired EBITA at 7x. Peer precedents: Sdiptech's ten divestments lifted its core margin to 21.5% and its shares by 42% in a year; Lifco's Dental mix shift to own manufacturing; Addtech's record 16% margin from mix and pricing; Indus's portfolio surgery pushed ROCE past 10%. Few big or many small: many small, which is both safer (no single point of failure) and slower (no single catalyst). Congruent with strategy: fully; every one of these is a stated priority.

What is not available is a step-change: there is no lever that takes the group margin from 9.4% to the 14% to 16% of the second-tier peers, because the portfolio's quality is the constraint. The mid-term output improvement is from about 9.4% to about 10% to 10.5% adjusted EBITA margin and from about SEK 0.95 to about SEK 1.25 to SEK 1.40 of adjusted EPS by 2028, which is what consensus already assumes.

C-79

Narrowness of the business perimeter, its change, and what it means for predictability

❌ D32

Question asked How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

Judgement: very wide and only slightly narrowing in product, widening in geography. 113 to 114 business units, about 400 legal entities, about 30 countries, three business areas, seven verticals (from 14); Sweden 45% of sales, euro area 18%, UK 11%, Norway 9%, Switzerland 4%; end-markets 48% industrial, 12% consumer discretionary, 8% consumer staples. Change: the consumer-retail exits and the vertical consolidation narrow the product perimeter; Singapore, Germany, Benelux, the UK and Switzerland widen the geographic one. What it means for predictability: low. The sector's own earnings review states that "earnings predictability varies significantly across the three business areas" and that forecasting needs "highly differentiated macro variables" per segment; the sum of 114 independent businesses is more stable than any one of them (the diversification argument management makes), but it is also opaque (no organic EBITA by vertical, no subsidiary cash flows), it is subject to unit-level surprises that surface late (fraud, impairment, a unit's chief executive leaving), and its reported results move with the krona, the Swiss franc and sterling without hedges. The width also dilutes the thesis: a correct call on the industrial cycle is worth only the Industry share of the group.

For the model: build three segments with separate drivers and seasonality, hold a line for unit-level surprises, and accept a wider forecast error than the group's diversification suggests.

Company - Management

❌ D4411 questions
C-5542C-5650C-5750C-57b38C-5845C-5942C-6055C-6148C-6228C-6345C-6440

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Storskogen's management is a competent repair team that has not yet shown it can be a compounding team. On the evidence of actions, the chief executive and chief financial officer have done what the situation required and said they would do: deleverage, divest, refinance, restore cash conversion, cut central costs, align incentives to the targets and restart acquisitions at disciplined prices and digestible sizes. On the evidence of outcomes, they have missed every stated profit target since listing, lost the head of their weakest segment within three months of appointing him, run a head office three to five times the size of the best peers', and operate without an internal audit across 114 units; the chief executive's ambition is to restore the model, his hiring record is mixed, his own equity is small, and the founders who presided over the 2021 to 2022 over-expansion still control the votes and chair the investment committee. Communication is clear and honest but unquantified, and the culture is in the second year of re-alignment rather than congruent. For the operating model, the Consumer should assume management delivers on the things it controls (interest cost, leverage below 2.5x, deal cadence rising, cash conversion above 70%, further pruning) and should not assume it delivers on the things the cycle controls (the 10% margin before 2028, 15% EBITA growth in any year without a large acquisition); the Services turnaround should be modelled as later and smaller than management implies until a permanent head is in place and two consecutive quarters above 10% are printed.

C-55

Size of ambition or vision, particularly against peers

❌ D42

Question asked How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

Judgement: the stated ambition is large (15% annual adjusted EBITA growth, a margin above 10%, organic growth above GDP plus 1 to 2 points, a 2024 Capital Markets Day with five megatrend themes) and the lived ambition is modest and defensive: a chief executive who "does not want to guide", whose archetype is an "operationally-focused caretaker" with partial scores on force, agency and dynamism (leadership-foundations verdict), who has spent two years retiring debt and selling businesses, and whose acquisitions since the restart are SEK 90m to SEK 130m of sales each. Against peers: Lifco's Waldemarson ("We don't spend much time following other companies"), Addtech's Stenberg, Lagercrantz's Wigh and Sdiptech's Mattson all run engines that deliver 15% to 20% EBITA growth; Volati's Stenbäck sets a quadrupling target he cannot fund. Storskogen's ambition is a restoration of the model rather than a push beyond it, which is appropriate to its situation and unremarkable as a force.

C-56

How great and smart the strategy is: moat-widening, growth priority, dynamism across environments

C50

Question asked How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

Judgement: smart as a repair strategy, not great as a compounding strategy. Right choices: the 7x cap (the single most important lesson of 2021 to 2022), B2B over consumer, non-Swedish over Swedish (Sweden is the rate-sensitive, construction-exposed end), themes with structural demand (automation, digitalisation, energy, health, infrastructure), cash conversion as a hard constraint, and verticals cut to seven. Bold choices: none; the strategy mirrors the peers' playbook and the targets are the ones set at the 2021 listing. Moat-widening: no, because the model has no moat to widen; the strategy improves portfolio quality at the margin by buying recurring-revenue units and selling price-takers. Growth as a priority: yes, from 2026, within leverage limits. Dynamism across environments: partial; the model proved it can retrench (2023 to 2025) and is now proving whether it can grow at 7x in a crowded market; in a renewed rate shock the strategy reverts to defence, which is the correct but unambitious response.

The strategy's one genuine strategic insight is the recognition that Storskogen's Swedish construction and consumer concentration is the problem and that geographic and B2B diversification is the fix; executing that at SEK 100m per deal into a SEK 33bn group will take a decade.

C-57

How great the chief executive is, judged by actions

C50

Question asked How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

Judgement: a competent operational stabiliser with a strong record in the repair phase and an unproven record as a capital allocator at group scale. Actions for: appointed interim in February 2024 after co-founder Daniel Kaplan's abrupt exit, made permanent in July 2024 after an external search; cut net debt by about SEK 4bn to 4.6bn to 2.3x; held cash conversion above 70% every year; sold nine loss-makers (SEK 110m of losses) and two consumer chains; cut parent costs by half; refinanced to 2029 and 2030; restarted acquisitions only when leverage allowed; reorganised verticals; put his own pay under a 15% EBITA-growth hurdle with no cash bonus in 2024 or 2025; communicates cautiously and honestly. Actions against: missed both 2025 targets; could not retain a Services head; runs a head office three to five times the size of the peers'; has a career concentrated in Nordic IT distribution and in building Trade (13 deals in 2021, including the consumer haircare units that have since been sold at a loss, which is a direct blot on his own allocation record); holds a "relatively minor" shareholding with no open-market purchases. The leadership-foundations verdict is "medium plausibility" of long-term execution, with strong scores on capital allocation and communication and partial scores on force, dynamism and outlier standards.

Judged by actions, he is the right chief executive for the phase just completed and an untested one for the phase just begun.

C-57b

Priority on hiring well, and the leadership team built

❌ D38

Question asked How high a priority is hiring well for the chief executive, and how great is the leadership team the chief executive has built (an enabler)?

Judgement: a weak point. The team: CFO Lena Glader (since 2019; ex-Eastnine CFO, ex-analyst; the strongest member on the evidence, delivering the deleveraging, refinancing and cash discipline); Head of Industry Fredrik Bergegård and Head of Trade Åsa Murphy (stable, Industry performing); Head of M&A Johan Ekström (succeeded co-founder Bjärgård in February 2025; now also interim Head of Services); UK Managing Director Chris Pullen (interim from May 2025, permanent February 2026; ex-Staffline and idverde chief executive; credible); Services: Peter Ahlgren (co-founder, 11 years) stepped back, Jesper Kronstrand (internal, had grown SoVent from SEK 130m to almost SEK 600m) lasted three months. The hiring record therefore shows one external success (Pullen), one internal failure (Kronstrand), a key-person concentration in the CFO, and the group's most important vacancy filled by the person who should be running the deal restart. Below the top, 54% of business-unit chief executives changed, about 45% of sellers remain active post-acquisition, and the group reports a "persistent shortage of qualified talent" outside metropolitan areas; a leadership framework programme and annual succession reviews exist. Peer benchmark: Addtech promotes almost exclusively from its own academy; Volati has an internal-promotion machine (15 of 22 chief executives internal); Sdiptech rebuilt its whole business-area layer in a year with external hires. Storskogen's bench is thinner than its size requires.

C-58

How well resourced and invested the whole business is (a buffer; built for resilience)

❌ D45

Question asked How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

Judgement: adequately resourced financially, under-resourced in control, and investing at a maintenance level. Financial buffer: leverage 2.4x against a 3.5x covenant, equity ratio 50%, no maturities until late 2027, cash SEK 1.4bn plus undrawn facilities, SEK 3.9bn of interest swaps (half short-dated). Investment: capex 1.6% of sales in 2025, 1.9% in H1 2026 and 2.3% in Q2 (growth investment in Industry, such as J&D Pierce's coating facility), in line with the 1.5% to 2.0% peer median; ERP rollouts, AI training, a Knowledge Exchange platform, central freight and energy agreements; a central team that "provides targeted operational, financial and sustainability support". Control: no internal audit across 114 units in 30 countries, decentralised self-assessment, 37 COSO key controls in smaller subsidiaries, a documented SEK 30m fraud in 2020, a 2022 subsidiary dispute recovering a third of a SEK 10m claim, and workplace accidents up to 83 from 66. The head office is large (79) but, per the sector research, short of investment managers. The "ant colony" test (built yesterday for resilience) is half-passed: the balance sheet is now resilient, the organisation is not demonstrably so.

C-59

Holistic, congruent culture, aligned across the total system

❌ D42

Question asked How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

Judgement: partially congruent, and less so than the peers the question cites. Congruent elements: decentralised entrepreneurship as the stated model; incentives now tied to the targets (15% EBITA growth, cash conversion) and settled in cash; a conservative dividend; a cautious communication style that matches a cautious strategy; high engagement at the centre (4.25 out of 5, 69.5% survey response). Incongruent elements: a 79-person centre coordinating 114 "autonomous" units (the model is neither Lifco-lean nor centrally run); a 2021 to 2022 culture of volume acquisition that had to be unlearned; 17% employee turnover and 54% unit-leader turnover; three Services heads in a year; a founder-controlled share structure alongside a professional chief executive with little equity; and expert-network evidence that rapid generalist acquisitions produced "minimal operational synergies". Against Lifco (margin-first, no HQ HR, managers told to ignore share) or Avanza or Ryanair, Storskogen's system is not yet symbiotic; it is a group in the second year of re-aligning goals, incentives and structure after a period in which they pulled apart.

C-60

Clarity and helpfulness of communication, including on strategy and the driver transmission mechanism

C55

Question asked How clear, straightforward and helpful is the company's communication, including on the strategy and the driver transmission mechanism?

Judgement: clear and honest, deliberately unquantified, and useful on direction more than on magnitude. Strengths: the targets are few and stable; management states plainly when they are missed ("achieving these targets will be challenging"), explains quarters in terms of named drivers (weather, construction, destocking, currency, the June finish), gives segment and vertical disclosure, explained the hedging-policy change and the FX transaction effects, and framed the capital-allocation pivot in a simple sequence (debt reduction to zero, cash to acquisitions, 7x or below, themes). The chief executive's phrases are consistent across calls and conferences. Weaknesses: no annual guidance by policy ("I don't want to guide"); no bridge from today's 9.4% margin and negative 3% EBITA growth to 10% and 15%; no organic EBITA by vertical, no subsidiary cash flows, no deal-by-deal multiples (the sector's analysts have to infer "7x or below"); a "strategic presentation framework that diverges from earnings-call realities by highlighting structural stability over cyclical construction headwinds" (leadership-foundations verdict). The transmission mechanism (cash flow to deals at 7x to EBITA growth) is stated but not evidenced with numbers the Consumer can model from. Communication is a "strong" score in the leadership-foundations archetype assessment; I would call it clear but thin.

C-61

Record of acting, not just talking: recent huge delivery and action, mid-term track record whatever the environment

❌ D48

Question asked How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?

Judgement: a strong record of action and a weak record of delivery against stated targets. Action: 2023, eleven divestments, SEK 2,759m of debt reduction, deal count cut from 54 to 12; 2024, nine loss-makers sold with a SEK 920m write-down, refinancing extending maturities to 33 months; 2025, SEK 900m to debt, a SEK 1bn bond refinanced to 2030 at 265bp over STIBOR from 687.5bp, Motavo sold, LEP bought; 2026, Perfect Hair sold, SEK 100m buyback, two platform deals in Q2 ahead of broker expectations, three more by October. This is a company that did what it said it would do in the repair phase, in a tough environment, and did it faster than the sell-side expected. Delivery against targets: the 2025 targets were missed on both counts (9.4% against above 10%; negative 3% against 15%), the 2021 listing targets have never been met, and DNB Carnegie's small-cap evaluation ranks it below Lifco, Addtech and Lagercrantz on hitting strategic objectives. Against estimates: beats in Q3 2024 (8%), Q4 2024, Q3 2025 (3%), Q2 2026 (2%); misses in Q1 2026 (3%); in line in Q4 2025. The pattern is delivery on the things management controls (cash, debt, disposals, refinancing) and non-delivery on the things the cycle controls (margin, growth), which is the defining feature of this management.

C-62

Outlier standards: higher, faster, better; record of out-executing competitors

❌ D28

Question asked To what extent does the company hold outlier standards, driving higher, faster and better, with a record of out-executing competitors?

Judgement: no. Every peer-relative measure places Storskogen at or near the bottom of the proven set: 2026 sales growth of 3% against 10% to 12% for Addtech, Lifco and Volati; EBITA margin 9.4% to 9.7% against 16% to 23%; ROCE 6.8% to 10% against 16.6% to 101%; ROE 6.1% against Lagercrantz's 23% median; organic EBITA negative 4% in H1 2026 against a positive 2% compounder median; the most active acquirer by count in 2022 to 2025 (63 deals) with the worst returns; the largest impairment in the peer group; head office three to five times the peers'. The one measure on which it has out-executed is the speed of deleveraging (SEK 4bn in three years). The leadership-foundations work scores outlier standards as partial, and the chief executive's own words ("once we start to deliver organic profit growth consistently") acknowledge that consistency has not yet been achieved. The 2026 Share Savings Programme's 15% hurdle sets an outlier standard on paper; it has not been met in any year since listing.

C-63

Shareholders first, ahead of management; prudence of governance; what the evidence shows and why

❌ D45

Question asked Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

Judgement: the evidence is mixed and leans toward founders first, management modest, and governance scaffolding incomplete. Shareholders first: the chief executive took no cash bonus in 2024 or 2025 and is paid SEK 14.6m (bottom quartile of premium peers; a fifth of Lifco's SEK 84.5m); the share savings programme is settled in cash to avoid dilution and requires 15% EBITA growth for full vesting; the dividend is a conservative 15% of net earnings and was raised; a SEK 100m buyback was completed; management cut its own head-office costs by half. Founders first: Class A shares carry ten votes, 7.4% of capital commands 44.5% of votes, the founders and partners hold 56% to 64% of votes on 40% of capital; co-founder Bjärgård chairs the investment committee as a dependent director; co-founder Ahlgren is a senior advisor; the structure insulates the controllers from minority oversight and removes a take-out floor. Prudence of governance: four of five directors independent (80%), an audit committee that met five times, EY as auditor since 2019 (SEK 27m plus SEK 12m to other auditors), COSO controls raised from 24 to 37, compliance training; but no internal audit function across 114 units (the board asserts self-assessment suffices), a SEK 30m fraud in 2020, insider dealing limited to incentive allotments with no open-market purchases, and the departure of the audit committee's ex-Hexagon CFO in May 2026. The scaffolding was put in place where it was needed after the fact (COSO, leverage discipline) and not where it has not yet been needed (internal audit), which is the opposite of the prudent pattern the question describes.

C-64

Enlightened capital allocator and dynamic repurchaser, with a record of high returns on new capital

❌ D40

Question asked To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

Judgement: not enlightened by the record, disciplined by the recent evidence, and not a dynamic repurchaser. The record: 152 acquisitions in 2020 to 2024, 119 in 2021 to 2022 at up to 10x profit against peers' 5x to 6x, financed to 4.35x leverage, followed by a SEK 920m impairment, SEK 1.5bn of sales sold at a loss, and ROCE of 6.8% to 10%; the leadership-foundations work calls it "aggressive historical acquisition pricing indiscipline", a 67% to 100% premium to peer norms. The recent evidence: two years of allocating free cash flow to debt (the right call), divestments at the right time, a 7x cap stated and apparently honoured, deals sized to be digestible, and a quarter-by-quarter cadence rather than a splurge. Repurchases: SEK 100m (0.5% of market capitalisation) at a price below 9x forward earnings, when the stock trades at a 30% to 50% discount to peers and the company's own deals at 7x EBITA imply its shares at 9x to 10x EV/EBITA are not obviously cheaper than targets; management has not mooted more, and the 15% payout plus deal priority leave no room. Return on new capital: at 7x, about 10% to 11% post-tax, above the cost of debt and around the cost of equity; the proven compounders earn more because they buy better businesses, not because they pay less.

The Consumer should credit the discipline since 2023 and discount the 2021 to 2022 record only partially: the same investment committee chair (Bjärgård) presided over both.

Risks

❌ D404 questions
C-8055C-8140C-8238C-8335

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The risk profile has changed shape rather than size over the last 18 months: the balance-sheet risk that dominated 2022 to 2024 has receded to tolerable (2.4x, maturities to 2027 and beyond, interest halved), and in its place sit three risks the Consumer must carry through the forecast. First, the engine-restart risk, that acquisitions do not reach a cadence that matters before the 12 to 24 month window closes and that organic EBITA stays negative, which would leave the shares as a cheap, slow-growing, construction-exposed conglomerate rather than a recovering compounder. Second, the control and earnings-quality risk inherent in 114 autonomous units with no internal audit, SEK 18bn of goodwill and a third of adjusted EPS made of amortisation, where failures surface late and large. Third, the sentiment risk of a small, illiquid, founder-controlled Swedish compounder in a rate-shock regime with one dominant covering broker, where a sector event or a seasonal miss can take 20% to 30% off the price without any change in fundamentals. The fundamental downside in a cyclical double-dip is a 25% to 30% EPS cut and the end of the acquisition programme, not insolvency; the valuation downside to a fair trough is 35% to 45%. Against these, the shares already trade at the sole broker's bear-case value, and the one risk that the question's wording would flag most (an engine decelerating from above trend) is absent, because the engine is accelerating from zero.

C-80

Deceleration of the core growth engine over the last twelve months: revenue growth against the company's normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing

C55

Question asked To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

Judgement: the core engine is re-accelerating from a stall rather than decelerating, on both of its cylinders, but both still run below the company's normal rate. Organic: negative 6% in Q1 2024, 0% in Q4 2024, about 2% in 2025 (DNB Carnegie's table says negative 4%; use the quarterly reports), 2% in Q1 2026, 5% in Q2 2026, against a target of GDP plus 1 to 2 points (roughly 3% to 4%) and a compounder median of 4% to 6%. Acquired: essentially zero in 2024, SEK 867m of spend in 2025 (nine deals), SEK 204m in H1 2026 on FactSet's cash-flow line with three platforms and three add-ons, and three more deals announced in July and October 2026, against a normal rate for a working compounder of 5% to 10% of sales a year; DNB Carnegie models 5% from 2027. Revenue: negative 5% in 2024, negative 3% in 2025, plus 3% to 4% expected in 2026, against the 5% of 2023 and the 96% of the 2021 to 2022 boom (not normal). The input change forces (industrial demand, interest cost, deal capacity) are all improving, not slowing; the one that is slowing is the krona-driven Trade margin tailwind, which is lagged and finite.

The risk the question probes is therefore inverted here: the danger is not that an engine running above trend slows, but that an engine restarting from zero fails to reach trend before the window (12 to 24 months) closes. The test is explicit: organic EBITA growth positive for two consecutive quarters (the risk report's progression test) and a visibly rising deal cadence by Q4 2026 and Q1 2027.

C-81

Sudden, high-magnitude, low-probability fundamental risks: plausibility and size

❌ D40

Question asked What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

Judgement: five, ranked by plausibility times magnitude. (1) A subsidiary-level control failure (fraud, misstatement, a failed acquisition requiring restructuring) in a group of 114 units with no internal audit: plausibility medium-low in any year, magnitude SEK 30m to SEK 300m (the 2020 precedent was SEK 30m; the sector precedent at Teqnion was 74% of net profit), and the damage would be to the multiple more than the earnings. (2) A goodwill and intangibles impairment: SEK 18.1bn to SEK 18.5bn of goodwill (43% of assets), one SEK 920m write-down already; plausibility medium over three years if Services does not recover; magnitude SEK 0.5bn to SEK 1.5bn, non-cash, but it moves the equity ratio and sentiment. (3) A simultaneous downturn in European industrial production and Nordic construction: DNB Carnegie's bear case is EBITA 15% below estimate, which takes leverage to about 2.8x to 3.0x, ends acquisitions, and under the 6% interest assumption compresses EPS by 25% to 30%; plausibility medium on a 12-month view ("medium" in the risk report), magnitude the largest. (4) A refinancing shock at the late-2027 maturities under a stressed credit regime (sub-investment-grade ratings, FFO to debt below 20% flagged by credit analysts): plausibility low given the 2028 to 2030 facilities already in place, magnitude SEK 50m to SEK 150m a year of extra interest. (5) A currency shock: a 10% krona depreciation against the euro helps reported EBITA by about 3% but hurts Trade margins with a two-quarter lag; a sharp Swiss franc move hits equity (SEK 44m per 1%); plausibility medium, magnitude moderate.

A sixth, more structural, is the "false friend" path the risk report names as the winning hypothesis: headline cash conversion and acquired growth masking organic EBITA decay, revealed late through earn-out reversals and declining contingent considerations (SEK 80m at June 2026); the tripwire is organic EBITA growth below negative 5% again.

C-82

Perception- or sentiment-driven sudden, high-magnitude risks, including sector or market events

❌ D38

Question asked What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

Judgement: high for this stock, because it is a small, illiquid (USD 4m to 5m traded a day; market capitalisation SEK 19bn), sub-investment-grade, founder-controlled Swedish compounder in a regime where the portfolio's Fragility tilt is Double Underweight and "the market refuses re-ratings". The specific events: (1) a sector de-rating on rates (Swedish compounders' multiples halved in 2022; the earnings-yield gap to swap rates narrowed in Q3 2026; three ECB rises priced by mid-2027; a Riksbank hike likely in November 2026); (2) contagion from a peer event (the unobtainable January 2026 short-seller note on Lifco drove it down 16%; Volati's collapse to negative 76% over twelve months; any peer impairment or fraud reads across to the name with the thinnest controls); (3) a Q3 miss on 23 October 2026 (Q1 and Q3 are the seasonally weak quarters, Q1 2026 missed by 3% and the stock had fallen 15.6% in the three months before that report); (4) a change in the sole detailed covering broker's stance (DNB Carnegie has cut its target four times in 2026, from SEK 18.0 to SEK 15.0, and is also the house that covers all the peers); (5) a Swedish country-level sell-off on the rate hike into the most rate-sensitive developed economy. The relative-strength percentile of 61 and a 12-month return of 16.7% give little cushion.

C-83

How far the shares could fall to a fair trough multiple

❌ D35

Question asked How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

Judgement: 35% to 45% from SEK 11, to SEK 6 to SEK 7, on a trough multiple applied to a cut earnings base; 25% to 30% on a trough multiple applied to current estimates.

The arithmetic. The 24-month-forward P/E is 9.26x today (77th percentile of the five-year range of 4.56x to 44.78x; 89th percentile of the last two years whose median is 8.33x); the dashboard's ten-year bands are 9.40x at the 90th percentile ("mania"), 8.50x at the 75th, 7.60x at the median, 7.10x at the 35th and 6.20x at the 10th ("crash"). On consensus 24-month-forward EPS of about SEK 1.16, the median multiple gives SEK 8.8 (negative 20%), the 35th percentile SEK 8.2 (negative 25%), the 10th percentile SEK 7.2 (negative 35%). In a downturn the EPS would be cut: a 15% EBITA shortfall with financial leverage takes adjusted EPS down 25% to about SEK 0.87; at 7.1x to 7.6x that is SEK 6.2 to SEK 6.6 (negative 40% to 44%), and at 6.2x it is SEK 5.4. DNB Carnegie's own bear case (EBITA 15% below, multiple 15% below 12x, so 8x EV/EBITA) gives SEK 11, which is where the shares already trade; that tells the Consumer that the broker's "bear" is the market's base. The 52-week low is SEK 7.94 and the two-year history shows the shares at SEK 8.37 in the last year. A fair trough, in my judgement, is about 7x the brokers' adjusted EPS (which is roughly 10x to 11x reported EPS and about 8x EV/EBITA), consistent with where the stock bottomed in 2023 to 2024 and with a sub-investment-grade, sub-10% ROCE profile, applied to an EPS of SEK 0.95 to SEK 1.05: SEK 6.7 to SEK 7.4.

Guidance

❌ D406 questions
C-6838C-7032C-7135C-7240C-8550C-7342

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Storskogen's guidance framework gives the Consumer very little to anchor on and should be treated as a statement of ambition rather than a forecast. There is no annual guidance by policy; the mid-term targets (15% EBITA growth 2025 to 2027, above 10% margin) have never been met, were not raised at the 2024 Capital Markets Day, and sit 35% to 40% above consensus 2027 EBITA; the mechanism is described clearly and the numbers are not; and the informal commentary is constructive on direction (order books, pipeline, cadence, margin "reasonable") without magnitude. The two visible brokers differ by 30% on 2026 EPS, so "consensus" is a thin, unstable median set by one house. For the model this means: build the forecast bottom-up from the three business areas and the deal cadence rather than from any company number; use consensus EBITA (flat 2026, plus 7% to 8% 2027) as the base case; use the 10% margin by 2028 as a reasonable central assumption and 15% EBITA growth as the bull bound; and recognise that because the company gives no anchor, estimate revisions after each quarter will be larger than at guided peers, in both directions.

C-68

Company guidance against sell-side consensus for this year and next, and why

❌ D38

Question asked To what extent is company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

Judgement: there is no annual guidance, and the mid-term targets sit well above consensus. Targets: adjusted EBITA growth averaging 15% a year for 2025 to 2027 and an adjusted EBITA margin above 10%. Consensus: EBITDA growth of 0.7% in 2026 and 5.4% in 2027 (SEK 4,174m and SEK 4,400m on the brokers' basis from SEK 4,144m in 2025); adjusted EBITA (DNB Carnegie) SEK 3,077m to SEK 3,181m in 2026 (flat to plus 2% on SEK 3,117m) and SEK 3,300m to SEK 3,437m in 2027 (plus 7% to 8%); consensus EBIT margin 7.0% in 2026 and 7.7% in 2027, implying adjusted EBITA margins of about 9.2% to 9.5% and 9.7% to 10.0%. So the target implies 2027 EBITA of about SEK 4.7bn (SEK 3,117m compounded at 15% for two years from a 2025 base that itself fell 3%) against consensus of about SEK 3.4bn, a gap of 35% to 40%; the margin target is met in consensus only at the top of the range in 2027 and more clearly in 2028 (8.2% EBIT margin, about 10.2% to 10.5% EBITA).

Why the gap: the sell-side does not believe the 15% target ("highly challenging", DNB Carnegie; the growth would require large acquisitions the balance sheet cannot fund at 2.5x) and treats it as aspirational; management has not withdrawn it but has conditioned it ("especially if the economic environment improves"). The two brokers also disagree with each other by 30% on 2026 EPS (SEK 1.11 against SEK 0.77), which says the "consensus" is two views, not a market. For the model: anchor on consensus EBITA, not on the target, and treat the target as the upper bound of the cone.

C-70

Record, nature and behaviour in upgrading guidance, beyond meeting or beating

❌ D32

Question asked Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

Judgement: there is no record of upgrading, because there is no guidance to upgrade; the mid-term targets were set at the 2021 listing, re-stated at the November 2024 Capital Markets Day, and have never been raised; they have been missed in every year for which they applied. The behaviour is the opposite of a sandbagger: a company that sets ambitious long-run targets, declines to forecast the year, and explains misses by external factors (weather, construction, currency, destocking) rather than resetting the targets. The chief executive's one quantified statement of intent ("acquisitions will step up quarter by quarter") is directional. Peer contrast: Indus raised its 2026 EBITA target by 45% in two steps (on a commodity windfall); Sdiptech set a 2030 EBITA ambition at its late-2025 Capital Markets Day; Lifco refuses guidance and delivers about 20% a year; Volati keeps a 15% target it has missed since 2021 and now needs to quadruple EBITA to catch up. Storskogen sits with Volati on target behaviour and with Lifco on guidance behaviour, without Lifco's delivery.

C-71

Conservatism of current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five, adjusted for the change in input forces

❌ D35

Question asked How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

Judgement: the targets are not conservative; they are ambitious against the record and achievable only with a large step-up in acquisitions. Record: adjusted EBITA SEK 3,238m in 2023, SEK 3,229m in 2024, SEK 3,117m in 2025 (negative 3%); EBITA growth of about 1%, 0% and negative 3%; margin 9.0%, 9.4%, 9.4%. Target: 15% a year and above 10%. Adjusting for the change in input forces: deleveraging is complete (so cash goes to deals instead of debt, worth perhaps 4% to 7% of EBITA a year), interest is lower (irrelevant to EBITA), the industrial cycle has turned (worth 2% to 4% of organic EBITA in Industry), Services may recover from a depressed base (worth 3% to 5% of group EBITA if the margin returns to 10%); together these could deliver 8% to 12% EBITA growth in a good year, which is the first time since 2022 that double-digit growth is plausible, but 15% averaged over 2025 to 2027 after a negative 3% start requires about 25% a year in 2026 and 2027, which is not in reach. The margin target is the more conservative of the two: a 10% margin is 60 basis points away and consensus has it by 2027 to 2028.

For the model: a 10% margin by 2028 is a reasonable base case; 15% EBITA growth is a bull case requiring SEK 3bn-plus of acquisitions a year at 7x.

C-72

Specificity of guidance: built from stated building blocks or vague about how results come about

❌ D40

Question asked How specific is the guidance: built from stated building blocks, or vague about how results come about?

Judgement: vague by policy on the numbers, specific on the mechanism. The company states no revenue, margin or EPS guidance for any year and no bridge from 9.4% to 10% or from negative 3% to 15%; it does state the building blocks qualitatively and consistently: debt reduction to near zero in 2026, free cash flow to acquisitions, platforms with EBITA of SEK 20m to SEK 50m at 7x or below within five themes, add-ons to existing platforms, B2B and non-Swedish bias, Services standardisation and digitalisation, pruning of sub-10% units, cash conversion above 70%, leverage below 2.5x. What is missing is the quantification that would let the Consumer check progress: acquired EBITA per year (Sdiptech states SEK 100m), deal multiples per transaction, organic EBITA by vertical, the Services margin path, and the net financial items run-rate (the interim report's SEK 20m per quarter saving is the one quantified block). DNB Carnegie fills the gap with its own assumptions (5% sales from M&A, 4% organic, margin 9.2% then 9.7%), and the market is modelling the broker, not the company.

C-85

Informal or qualitative commentary against formal guidance and consensus, and why

C50

Question asked To what extent is informal/qualitative company commentary above or below formal guidance and sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

Judgement: informal commentary is mildly above consensus on direction and silent on magnitude, and it is the only forward signal the company gives. The qualitative statements since the Q2 report (11 August 2026): "cautiously optimistic" on the second half, "solid order books in Industry", "good opportunities to continue adding acquired growth", "the pace of capital allocated to acquisitions will step up quarter by quarter", "our target of at least 10% is reasonable, especially if the economic environment improves", "early positive signs in the consumer climate" tempered by "the third quarter remains a seasonally weaker period ... characterised by customer restraint", and management's expectation that second-half cash conversion improves as Q2 receivables settle. Consensus (two brokers) has 2026 revenue up 3.4%, EBITDA flat, margin flat, and 2027 EBITA up 7% to 8%: management's tone implies a better 2027 than that if the deal cadence and the Services recovery arrive, and a worse Q3 than the run-rate implies on seasonality. The reason the commentary runs above consensus is that management is describing capacity (cash available, pipeline, order books) while the sell-side models delivery (deals closed, margins printed); the gap between capacity and delivery is the company's track record, and the sell-side is right to price it.

C-73

What else is material or uncommon about guidance or investor relations

❌ D42

Question asked What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

Judgement: five things. (1) Coverage is thin and concentrated: FactSet holds two visible brokers (DNB Carnegie, four analysts over time, the only house publishing detailed post-results models; Nordea), SEB and others attend calls but do not publish accessible notes, and DNB Carnegie also covers every peer and advises some, so one house's framework sets the narrative. (2) The two brokers disagree by 30% on 2026 EPS and by 21% on 2027, which makes FactSet's median and mean unstable (median EPS 1.03, 1.25, 1.22; mean 0.978, 1.16, 1.24) and the 2028 "decline" an artefact. (3) The company discontinued all currency hedging from 2026 and says so plainly, which is uncommon among Nordic peers (Addtech mandates hedging above 5% of budgeted profit) and makes reported figures follow the krona. (4) Seasonality is strong and management asks to be judged on rolling twelve months: Q1 and Q3 margins of about 8% to 9.5%, Q2 and Q4 of about 9.7% to 10%; Q3 2026 reports on 23 October 2026. (5) The founders' investment committee chairmanship and the cash-settled incentive programme are disclosed but under-discussed; the lack of organic EBITA by vertical and of subsidiary cash flows is the data gap every piece of research names.
