Focus: What most needs answering is the reconciliation from adjusted EBITA to adjusted and reported earnings per share and to free cash flow available for acquisitions in 2026 to 2028, line by line, with the two brokers' assumptions set against company disclosure, so that the second-set Analyst can see why the brokers' 2026 earnings per share forecasts differ by about 30% and which lines carry the forecast risk.

Layer 2

Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

9 questions in 1 group · 13,037 words · written 23 October 2026, 02:31 to 02:48 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Storskogen (STOR.B-SE): second set of questions, translating the foundations into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months to four years

Written 2026-10-22, the day before the Q3 2026 report (23 October 2026). Money in SEK unless stated. Fiscal year is the calendar year; the company reports quarterly.

Orientation for the Consumer (read before the answers)

Three things set the frame for everything below.

First, the base. 2025 on the brokers' basis: net sales SEK 33,097m, adjusted EBITDA SEK 4,144m (12.5%), adjusted EBITA SEK 3,117m (9.4%), so depreciation excluding amortisation of acquired intangibles (and including the depreciation of right-of-use assets) is SEK 1,027m, 3.1% of sales; amortisation of acquired intangibles about SEK 700m (DNB Carnegie: SEK 683m for 2026); net financial items SEK 828m; tax 24.4%; reported EPS SEK 0.63; brokers' adjusted EPS SEK 0.953. Quarterly adjusted EBITA: Q1 2025 about SEK 700m (8.8%), Q2 2025 about SEK 845m (10.0%), Q3 2025 SEK 759m (9.5%), Q4 2025 SEK 816m (9.4%), Q1 2026 SEK 639m (8.1%), Q2 2026 SEK 864m (9.7%). The first half of 2026 delivered sales of SEK 16,712m (+2.0%) and adjusted EBITA of SEK 1,503m (-3% on the first half of 2025), with organic EBITA -4%.

Second, the consensus trap, which is larger than the first set recorded. FactSet's "consensus" EPS of SEK 1.03 (2026), SEK 1.25 (2027) and SEK 1.22 (2028) is the median of two brokers on two different bases. DNB Carnegie (SEK 1.11, 1.25, 1.36) adds back amortisation of acquired intangibles (SEK 683m in 2026, SEK 0.31 a share after tax). Nordea (SEK 0.77, 1.03, 1.22) does not: its 2026 figure sits exactly where the company's own adjusted EPS lands after amortisation (first-half 2026 company adjusted EPS of SEK 0.36 plus a seasonally stronger second half of about SEK 0.42 gives SEK 0.78). The 30% gap between the brokers is therefore mostly a definitional gap, not a disagreement about the business; FactSet's EPS median is a blend of two bases and should not be used for anything. DNB Carnegie's EBITA, Nordea's EBITDA (SEK 4,154m for 2026, within 3% of DNB Carnegie's SEK 4,036m) and the revenue lines are comparable and should be used instead. This also reverses one reading in the first set: the 21% consensus EPS growth for 2027 is largely a median artefact; on DNB Carnegie's single basis 2027 growth is 12.6% (SEK 1.11 to SEK 1.25), and most of the interest step-down lands in 2026, not 2027.

Third, the shape of my base case, so the Consumer can see where each answer feeds it. Revenue growth +2.0% in 2026 (organic +3.5%, acquired +1.5%, divestments -1.7%, currency -1.3%), +6.3% in 2027, +8.0% in 2028 and 2029, +7.5% in 2030, with acquisitions of SEK 1.6bn to SEK 2.3bn a year at 7x EBITA as the main driver from 2027. Adjusted EBITA margin 9.3% in 2026, 9.6% in 2027, 9.9% in 2028, 10.0% in 2029, 10.1% in 2030 (brokers' EBITDA margin 12.4%, 12.6%, 12.9%, 13.0%, 13.1%). Net financial items SEK 580m to SEK 650m. Tax 25%. Adjusted EPS on DNB Carnegie's basis SEK 1.12, 1.27, 1.44, 1.58, 1.73; reported EPS SEK 0.76, 0.91, 1.05, 1.16, 1.28. Free cash flow after interest, tax, capital spending and lease payments, before acquisitions and dividends, SEK 1.6bn rising to SEK 2.4bn. Leverage drifts down from 2.4x to about 2.1x even with the acquisition cadence stepping up, which is the single most important finding of this set: the balance sheet is no longer the constraint on the engine; organisational capacity and deal flow are.

Where I disagree with the first set I say so inside the relevant answer; the disagreements are listed together at the end of P-10.

Financial forecasts

C569 questions
P-0155P-0258P-0342P-0444P-0556P-0646P-0858P-0955P-1056

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The financial forecasts for Storskogen over the next 18 months to four years describe a company that has finished being a balance-sheet story and is becoming, slowly and from a low base, a growth story funded by its own cash: adjusted EBITA from SEK 3.1bn to SEK 4.5bn by 2030 (8% to 10% a year after a flat 2026), an adjusted EBITA margin from 9.3% to 10.1%, adjusted EPS from SEK 0.95 to SEK 1.73 (12% a year, with 2026's 17% front-loaded by the interest step-down) and reported EPS from SEK 0.63 to SEK 1.28, free cash flow after leases from SEK 1.6bn to SEK 2.4bn, and leverage drifting down to about 2.1x even as acquisition spending rises to SEK 2.5bn a year. The growth is bought more than earned: about 55% of the EBITA growth comes from acquisitions at 7x, a quarter from organic volume and mix, the rest from one banked finance step-down and from margin levers that are small (cost 30 to 40 basis points, pricing nil net of cost, currency a two-year windfall in Trade). Operating leverage is real but confined to Services and Product Solutions and worth SEK 100m to SEK 150m; financial leverage is a mild and fading amplifier; the feedback loops net out positive because the acquisition loop is self-funding at this cadence and the balancing loops (amortisation, put liabilities, goodwill, thin controls) cap reported earnings and raise tail risk without stopping the engine. The track record says to lean below management on everything the cycle controls and in line with DNB Carnegie through 2027, then above it, and to set Nordea's EPS aside as a different basis. For the Consumer's predictability work the drivers are concentrated, not diffuse: the deal cadence (observable, management-controlled, the main upside), the Services margin (observable, cycle-controlled, the main downside) and the Q3 and Q1 seasonal prints (the main near-term volatility), with net financial items, tax, capital spending and the dividend now among the most predictable lines in the model. The cone is wide, -10% to +8% on 2027 EBITA and SEK 1.30 to SEK 2.20 on 2030 adjusted EPS, with a slightly negative skew into the next two prints and a slightly positive skew beyond, because the one thing the base case leaves on the table, SEK 1.5bn to SEK 2.5bn of unused leverage capacity by 2028, is a real option in management's hands. On these forecasts the shares at 9.8x 2026 and 8.7x 2027 adjusted EPS (14.5x and 12.1x reported) are priced for the engine not to restart; the forecasts say it has, at a pace that is modest, cash-funded and, for the first time since 2022, not threatened by the balance sheet.

P-01

Fixed-cost operating leverage: how it shapes the main line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years

C55

Question asked After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

Judgement: Storskogen's operating leverage is low at the group level and high in two pockets, and over the next 18 months to four years it works mildly in the company's favour for the first time since 2022: a one-point move in organic volume moves adjusted EBITA by about SEK 70m to SEK 100m (2% to 3%) within the year and about SEK 50m (1.5%) once local headcount has adjusted, which is two to three times the SEK 31m "proportional" sensitivity the sell-side quotes for a 1% sales move. The leverage is worth roughly 30 to 40 basis points of group margin over 2026 to 2028 (SEK 100m to SEK 150m of EBITA) if Services and the Product Solutions vertical recover a few points of volume, and it would cost the same again in a renewed downturn. It is not the lever that takes the margin to 10%; mix, divestments and acquired units do most of that. Against the last four to six years, the difference is one of direction, not magnitude: 2023 to 2025 showed negative leverage (Services EBITA -30% to -36% on flat sales in Q1 2026, Product Solutions under-utilised, industrial volumes down about 20% from 2022), and 2026 to 2028 should show the same mechanism running the other way at about the same drop-through.

What the thing is. The cost base in the second quarter of 2026 was raw materials and consumables 55.1% of sales (Trade 63.8%, Industry 54.6%, Services 46.8%), personnel 22.9%, other external 10.0%, depreciation excluding acquired intangibles about 3.0%, leaving an adjusted EBITA margin of 9.7%. Materials are variable within the quarter. Personnel is semi-fixed: Swedish notice periods and the project nature of Services mean a volume fall lands on margin for one to three quarters before headcount follows (Services cut about 900 employees, from 4,328 to 3,411, between late 2023 and late 2024, and Q4 2024 Services EBITA then rose 32%). Other external costs are partly discretionary (down 4% in Q2 2026). Central costs are small (parent administrative expenses SEK 167m) and flat. So the structural drop-through on a volume change is the gross margin (35% to 40% on the company's own description, before the semi-variable personnel line) in the first quarters and roughly 20% once labour adjusts. The sell-side's "1% of sales moves EBITA by SEK 31m" is a price-and-mix-neutral sensitivity (1% of sales times the 9.4% margin), and it understates volume leverage by a factor of two to three. I disagree with the first set's C-75 reading that this implies "a largely variable cost base" and "close to proportional" leverage; the first set's own evidence (Industry's incremental margin of 21% in Q2 2026 and 18% for the half, Services' 30% EBITA fall on flat sales) is the better guide, and I use it.

Where the fixed costs sit, and why that matters for the model. Two places carry under-absorbed capacity today: Infrastructure Services (construction-exposed contracting and installation units, about SEK 1.4bn to SEK 1.5bn of quarterly sales, with the group's 20% to 25% construction exposure) and Product Solutions in Industry (about SEK 2.2bn of half-year sales, flat, with factories that management says run at low utilisation because of indirect consumer and construction exposure). Together these are roughly SEK 10bn of annual sales, 30% of the group. The rest of the group (Industrial Technology and Automation project companies, Trade distribution, Business Services) is close to variable, with drop-through of 12% to 20%. The arithmetic of a recovery: if the under-utilised SEK 10bn grows 4% in volume over two years with a 30% drop-through, EBITA rises by about SEK 120m (40 basis points of group margin); if the other SEK 23bn grows 3% a year at 15%, that adds about SEK 100m a year, which is roughly what price and cost inflation absorb. So the "leverage" the market can see over 18 months is concentrated in Services and Product Solutions, and it is worth about SEK 100m to SEK 150m, not the SEK 300m-plus that a naive application of a 35% gross margin to all organic growth would suggest.

Its trend and the change in its trend. 2023 to 2025 was a period of negative operating leverage masked by price and divestments: management says industrial and trading volumes fell about 20% from the 2022 peak while sales were held at SEK 33bn by price, and organic EBITA fell 12% in Q1 2026 and 4% for the first half. The turn in trend is visible in Industry (organic EBITA +12% in Q2 2026 on 6% organic sales growth, margin 11.0% from 10.4%, incremental margin 21%), not yet in Services (margin 10.3% in Q2 2026 from 10.8%, and 7.7% in Q1) or Trade (organic EBITA -8% in the first half). The group's twelve-month margin at 9.2% is below both 2024 and 2025, so leverage is still net negative on a trailing basis; my base case has it turning net positive from Q4 2026 and contributing from 2027.

Predictability and persistence. Low predictability on timing, because the two pockets depend on Swedish construction (housing starts about 8,000 against 18,000 in 2021; a Riksbank hike likely in November 2026; European swap rates up 70 basis points in August and September) and on Nordic consumer demand, neither of which has turned; moderate predictability on magnitude, because the mechanism is simple and the company has demonstrated both directions. Persistence is cyclical: the leverage works until capacity is re-absorbed (two to three years of 3% to 5% volume growth from here) and then stops; it is not structural.

How it shapes the line items, in my base case. Revenue: operating leverage does not touch the revenue line, but the fact that most 2024 to 2026 organic growth was price means the volume base is lower than the sales base suggests, which is why I hold organic growth at 3% to 3.5% rather than extrapolate Q2's 5%. Gross margin: stable to slightly up as Trade's import costs fall with the krona. Adjusted EBITA margin: 9.3% in 2026 (a weak Q1 and a seasonally weak Q3 already in the base), 9.6% in 2027 and 9.9% in 2028, of which I attribute about 20 basis points in 2027 and 20 in 2028 to volume leverage in Services and Product Solutions, the rest to mix (acquired units at 10% to 12% margins, consumer exits), Trade's currency tailwind and Industry pricing. EBITDA follows EBITA plus depreciation at 3.0% to 3.1% of sales. EPS: with net financial items of about SEK 580m against EBITA of SEK 3.1bn to SEK 3.4bn, financial leverage multiplies each 1% of EBITA into about 1.2% of pre-tax profit, so the SEK 100m to SEK 150m of operating leverage is worth 4% to 6% of adjusted EPS by 2028. Free cash flow: operating leverage improves cash conversion only after the working-capital build that accompanies volume (receivables rose SEK 378m in Q2 2026 on a strong June), so the cash benefit lags the profit benefit by two quarters.

The range it creates, which the Consumer should carry into the predictability work. On a SEK 34bn to SEK 36bn revenue base, plus or minus three points of organic volume concentrated in the two cyclical pockets moves adjusted EBITA by roughly SEK 200m to SEK 300m (6% to 9%) and adjusted EPS by 8% to 12% before financial leverage, 10% to 15% after it. That is the operating half of the cone; the acquisition cadence (P-05) is the other half. The downside case is the more mechanical of the two: a renewed Nordic construction leg down in 2027 (the Riksbank hiking into a rate-sensitive mortgage market) would take Services back toward an 8% to 9% full-year margin and cost SEK 100m to SEK 150m against my base, and no central cost programme exists to offset it (see P-03).

Peers, and why Storskogen differs. Lifco expanded its margin by 1.6 points in 2020 on -0.5% organic sales; Addtech printed a record 16% margin on flat organic sales; Indutrade's margin fell from 12.1% to 9.5% in 2009 on -10% organic sales and recovered to 10.4% in 2010 on +14%. Storskogen's Services behaves like Indutrade in 2009, not like Lifco, because it is labour-intensive, construction-exposed and lacks subcontractor flexibility (Instalco runs subcontractors at 14% to 16% of costs; Bravida's cost base is 42% fully flexible). The lesson for the model is that Storskogen's margin will follow volume more closely than the premium peers' margins do, in both directions, and that this is a feature of its mix rather than of its management.

P-02

Financial leverage: how it shapes EPS and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years

C58

Question asked After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: financial leverage is now a modest, one-directional amplifier rather than a threat, and its contribution to EPS is front-loaded and finite. The interest step-down from SEK 828m in 2025 to about SEK 580m in 2026 is already three-quarters banked (first-half 2026 net financial items SEK 277m against SEK 420m a year earlier) and adds about SEK 0.11 to adjusted EPS, roughly two-thirds of the 2026 adjusted EPS growth on a single basis (SEK 0.953 to SEK 1.12); after 2026 the line is flat to slightly rising (SEK 575m in 2027, SEK 590m in 2028, SEK 620m in 2029, SEK 650m in 2030) as acquisition-funded net debt grows and the November 2026 Riksbank hike lands on the unhedged floating portion, so financial leverage contributes nothing to EPS growth in 2027 to 2030 and subtracts one to two points a year. The balance sheet, by contrast, improves through the horizon even at a stepped-up acquisition cadence: net debt rises from about SEK 9.1bn at end-2026 to about SEK 12bn by 2030 while EBITDA rises faster, so leverage falls from 2.3x to 2.4x today to about 2.1x, leaving SEK 1.5bn to SEK 2.5bn of unused capacity to the 2.5x ceiling by 2028. That unused capacity is the cone's upside (a larger platform, or buybacks at a 9x multiple) and the case's shock absorber; it means a 15% EBITA shortfall takes leverage to about 2.6x to 2.8x rather than toward the 3.5x covenant.

The mechanics, line by line. Interest-bearing net debt was SEK 9,896m at June 2026 (the company's figure, which includes lease liabilities, against adjusted EBITDA of SEK 4,144m, 2.4x). Of gross debt of about SEK 9.7bn (plus about SEK 1.6bn of leases), roughly SEK 3.9bn was swapped at end-2025 (average fixed rate 2.78%), with SEK 2.0bn of swaps expiring within a year, so about SEK 6bn to SEK 7bn floats on STIBOR; the company's own sensitivity is SEK 69m of pre-tax profit per 100 basis points (the prospectus said SEK 56m, so the unhedged share has risen as swaps expired). Bond 1 (SEK 1,250m at STIBOR plus 375 basis points) matures in late 2027; the 2025/2030 bond of SEK 1,000m is at STIBOR plus 265 basis points; the revolving credit facility runs to 2029 and the term loan to late 2028; there are no maturities in 2026. The blended cost of debt is therefore about 5.5% today (STIBOR about 2.5% plus margins of 2.65% to 3.75% on bonds and less on bank debt). DNB Carnegie models 6% on net debt, which is above the run-rate and is why its net financial items are above mine and why there is modest upside to its EPS from this line in 2026.

How I build the line. 2026: SEK 277m in the first half, SEK 300m to SEK 305m in the second (a 25 to 50 basis point Riksbank move from November adds SEK 5m to SEK 10m in the quarter; FX losses in financial items of the kind seen in 2025, SEK 42m, are a wildcard I carry at SEK 20m), total SEK 580m. 2027: the full-year effect of the hike (SEK 20m to SEK 35m) is offset by the late-2027 refinancing of Bond 1 at a lower spread (a 100 basis point lower margin on SEK 1.25bn saves SEK 12m a year, from Q4) and by lower average net debt in the first half; SEK 575m. 2028 to 2030: net debt rises by about SEK 0.6bn to SEK 0.8bn a year as acquisitions exceed free cash flow after dividends (see P-05), at 5.5% to 6%, so SEK 590m, SEK 620m, SEK 650m. A rating upgrade (Moody's B1, S&P BB stable; FFO to debt above 20% is the trigger) would cut 50 to 100 basis points off the next bond, worth SEK 10m to SEK 25m a year from 2028; I do not include it.

What financial leverage does to EPS over the horizon. The amplifier: with EBITA of SEK 3.1bn to SEK 4.5bn and net financial items of SEK 0.58bn to SEK 0.65bn, interest cover rises from 5.4x in 2026 to 7x by 2030, so a 10% change in EBITA moves adjusted pre-tax profit by 12% in 2026 and 11% by 2030. That is a mild amplifier by the standards of the sector's 2022 to 2023 experience (when cover was below 3x) and it fades. The step-down: 2025 to 2026 net financial items fall SEK 248m, which at a 25% tax rate on 1,678m shares is SEK 0.11 of adjusted EPS, against total growth in my base case of SEK 0.17 (SEK 0.953 to SEK 1.12); the remaining SEK 0.06 is EBITA (flat to +0.5%), tax and share count. From 2027 the line is a small headwind: SEK 575m to SEK 650m costs about SEK 0.01 of EPS a year as net debt grows. The sensitivities to carry: 100 basis points on STIBOR is SEK 69m pre-tax, SEK 0.03 of EPS (2.7% of 2026); a refinancing of Bond 1 at today's spread rather than a lower one makes no difference; a refinancing in a stressed market at 2023-style spreads (687 basis points at the peak) would cost SEK 40m a year on that bond alone, which is the low-probability tail.

What it does to the balance sheet. End-2025 net debt (company basis) about SEK 9.5bn; my path: about SEK 9.1bn at end-2026 (free cash flow of SEK 1.6bn after leases, acquisitions and minority buyouts of about SEK 0.65bn, dividend SEK 0.19bn, new leases about SEK 0.3bn), 2.2x; about SEK 9.7bn at end-2027 (acquisitions SEK 1.85bn), 2.15x; SEK 10.5bn at end-2028, 2.1x; SEK 11.4bn at end-2029, 2.1x; SEK 12.2bn at end-2030, 2.05x. Equity rises from about SEK 20.5bn toward SEK 27bn as retained earnings outrun the 15% payout, so the equity ratio holds near 50% (the Swiss franc, 22% of net assets on 4% of sales, moves it by SEK 44m per 1%, which is noise). Goodwill rises with each deal (78% of purchase price in the 2026 combinations), from SEK 18.1bn to about SEK 24bn by 2030, so the share of capital employed that is goodwill stays above 40% and ROCE improves slowly (10% to about 12%) rather than fast. Minority put liabilities (SEK 1,740m at June 2026, up from SEK 1,473m including a SEK 205m remeasurement) are debt-like and I treat the cash to settle them (about SEK 200m to SEK 300m a year) inside the acquisitions line; they are the one balance-sheet item that grows with subsidiary performance rather than with deals, and the Consumer should ask where the remeasurement is booked (equity or financial items) because the company's disclosure does not make it clear and it affects reported EPS.

The judgement that matters for the predictability work. The first set called leverage "the largest single amplifier in the case" (C-76). I agree for 2026 and disagree for 2027 to 2030: once the step-down is in the base, the interest line becomes one of the most predictable lines in the model (floating exposure known, maturities known, spreads known), and the amplifier is small. The balance-sheet finding is the more important one: on my cadence, leverage falls through the horizon, which means (a) the "window closes when leverage nears its ceiling" pattern from the sector digest does not close within four years at SEK 1.6bn to SEK 2.3bn of annual spend, and (b) management has the room to spend SEK 3bn a year (which would lift leverage to about 2.5x by 2028 and add roughly 4% a year to EBITA growth) or to buy back SEK 0.5bn to SEK 1bn of stock a year under the SEK 1,337m authorisation. It has said it will do neither. The Consumer should model the base case on the stated cadence and treat the spare capacity as the identified upside, because it is where a positive surprise to EPS would come from.

P-03

Cost elimination and efficiency: how much reaches the main line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when

❌ D42

Question asked After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

Judgement: little, and late. There is no group cost programme, there will not be one (the decentralised model precludes it and management has not announced one), and the central cost base (SEK 167m of parent administrative expenses, a 79-person head office) offers at most SEK 50m to SEK 80m of savings that management has shown no intention of taking. What reaches the line items is unit-level and structural: Services standardisation and digitalisation (SEK 50m to SEK 100m over 2027 to 2028, 50 to 100 basis points on a SEK 9.5bn segment), procurement frame agreements (SEK 20m banked, perhaps SEK 20m to SEK 30m more), ERP consolidation in Trade and Industry (cost now, benefit after 2028), and pruning of sub-10% units (margin-accretive by 10 to 30 basis points if done, with a capital loss each time). In total about SEK 100m to SEK 150m of EBITA, or 30 to 40 basis points of group margin, arriving mostly in 2027 and 2028, which is similar in size to the operating-leverage contribution and together with it accounts for the margin path from 9.3% to 9.9%. The cost lever is defensive: it is what will protect the margin in a downturn (local managers "cut variable costs instantly", about 900 Services employees cut in 2024), not a source of upside, and the Consumer should treat any claim of a step-change in costs as inconsistent with the model.

What is cuttable, and what the record says. The centre: parent administrative expenses fell from SEK 307m to SEK 167m between 2023 and 2025, partly by transferring intra-group services into Storskogen Management AB (cost-neutral at group level), so the genuine saving at the centre is smaller than the parent line suggests. A head office of 79 against Lifco's 16 and Indutrade's 24 is a 230% to 395% staffing premium, but the sector research also notes that Storskogen was left with too few central investment managers after its growth, and the M&A restart needs more of them, not fewer. I assume central costs flat in nominal terms (SEK 170m to SEK 180m a year), which is a small real reduction. The units: personnel is the cuttable line (Services cut about 20% of its workforce in a year and Q4 2024 Services EBITA rose 32%), external costs flex (down 4% in Q2 2026), materials follow volume. These cuts are defensive and reactive, done by local managers after volumes fall; they do not produce a margin above the pre-downturn level, and they were largely done in 2023 to 2024. Amortisation, leases, earn-outs and minority buyouts are not cuttable, and amortisation rises with every deal.

What is reducible structurally, with timing. (1) Services operational excellence: the stated top priority, led by an interim head who is also Head of M&A. The content is standardisation of processes, shared digital tools (AI for local pricing, group ERP), consolidation of overlapping units (verticals cut from 14 to 7), and selective bidding (the segment opted out of low-margin tenders in 2025, cutting sales 17% in Q2 2025 to protect margin). I put the benefit at 50 to 100 basis points on Services over two years, SEK 50m to SEK 100m, starting in the second half of 2027 once a permanent head is in place and the construction price war eases; nothing in 2026. (2) Procurement: SEK 20m banked (Vattenfall energy, DHL, PostNord, a central freight framework); the former Lifco acquisitions head's evidence is that decentralised groups deliberately avoid central purchasing, so I add SEK 20m to SEK 30m over three years, not more. (3) ERP and digitalisation in Trade and Industry: cost of SEK 20m to SEK 40m a year in 2026 to 2027 (inside other external costs), benefit of similar size from 2029. (4) Pruning: Motavo and Perfect Hair removed about SEK 560m of sales at a negative or sub-5% margin, worth about 10 to 15 basis points of group margin in 2026; a further round on sub-10% Services units would add 10 to 30 basis points but is not announced, so it is in my cone, not my base. (5) The krona and Trade's input costs are a pricing matter, not a cost programme, and are handled in P-04.

Why the structure caps the lever. The AlphaSense work is clear on the mechanism: decentralised groups "deliberately avoid centralising operational functions" to preserve entrepreneurship; the cost base is 114 separate decisions. Lifco runs the model with 16 central staff and a margin-first culture that tells managers to ignore share; Storskogen is trying to install the culture (incentives now on EBITA growth and cash conversion, unit chief executives changed at a 54% rate) but cannot install a cost programme. Sdiptech's precedent (ten divestments took its core margin to 21.5%) is a portfolio action, not a cost action, and Storskogen's equivalent is largely done. Volati's S:t Eriks and Tornum show the other side: production cuts and cost adaptation protect cash but leave under-absorption until volume returns. The reading for Storskogen is that its margin improvement is bought through mix (what it buys and sells), not through cost.

How it reaches the line items in my base case. Other external costs: flat at about 10% of sales, with ERP and compliance spending (CSRD, FDI screening, "melt and pour" steel traceability from October 2026) offsetting procurement gains. Personnel: 23% of sales, rising slightly in 2026 (Swedish wage agreements running about 3% a year against 2% organic volume), then stable as Services productivity improves. Central costs: SEK 170m to SEK 180m. Adjusted EBITA margin: the cost lever contributes about 10 basis points in 2026 (pruning), 15 in 2027 and 15 in 2028 (Services productivity, procurement), zero thereafter unless a new round of pruning or a head-office reduction is announced. Items affecting comparability below adjusted EBITA: I carry SEK 60m a year of restructuring, remeasurement and divestment charges (SEK 28m in Q2 2026 alone; SEK 65m capital loss on Perfect Hair in Q1), which the cost agenda generates rather than removes. Cash: the cost agenda is cash-neutral to slightly negative in 2026 to 2027 (ERP, digital tools, central investment managers for M&A), positive from 2028.

When the Consumer should change this view. Upward, if a permanent Services head is appointed with a quantified programme (Sdiptech-style dated targets), or if management announces head-office reductions or a second pruning round; each would be worth 10 to 30 basis points. Downward, if Services needs another workforce cut in 2027 (a renewed construction leg down), because the cut protects cash but the severance and under-absorption would push the segment margin back to 8% for two to three quarters first.

P-04

Pricing power: how price contributes to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years

❌ D44

Question asked After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: price will contribute about 1.5 to 2 points a year to organic revenue growth over the horizon (half or more of the 3% to 3.5% organic growth I model) and roughly nothing to the group margin, because Storskogen's pricing is cost recovery with a lag, not real pricing: Industry recovers cost in one quarter, Trade in two to four quarters (and is currently enjoying a windfall from a stronger krona worth 50 to 100 basis points on its margin for 2026 to 2027), and construction-exposed Services cannot recover cost at all until volumes return. Over 2024 to the first half of 2026 price did most of the work on the revenue line (management says pricing held sales at SEK 33bn through a 20% volume fall; the AlphaSense work reads Q2 2026's 5% organic growth as largely price), which means the margin stayed flat while volume fell: that is the signature of a price-taker that passes cost through. The next four years invert the mix (volume contributes more, price less) without changing the margin conclusion. The one place price moves the margin is Trade's currency effect, which is positive now, worth SEK 50m to SEK 100m in 2026 to 2027, and will reverse whenever the krona weakens because hedging was discontinued from 2026.

What the evidence shows, by condition. (1) Input-cost inflation: Industry "successfully implemented strategic price increases" against raw materials in Q2 2026 (materials up 8% against 5% sales growth, Industry margin 11.0% from 10.4%); the 2022 annual report shows the same pattern with a half-year lag; this is pass-through, with the margin preserved rather than expanded. Services could not pass through material and fuel costs in Q2 2026 (margin 10.3% from 10.8%). (2) Wage inflation: personnel is 46% of Services' costs; the Swedish wage round runs about 3% a year; framework agreements in Services are "fixed price per quantity" with negligible variable elements, so wage inflation lands on margin until contracts roll, which in road and infrastructure contracts can be years (DNB Carnegie notes long-term road contracts with limited ability to influence price). (3) Construction downturn: "intense industry-wide price pressure" since 2023; the segment's response was to decline low-margin tenders (sales -17% in Q2 2025) and to cut headcount; price has been given back, not held, in the units that compete for private construction work. (4) Consumer destocking: Trade's health and beauty units could not raise prices into retailers optimising inventory; organic EBITA -8% in the first half of 2026. (5) Currency: hedges were discontinued for 2026; the Trade lag is two to four quarters because inventory must clear; transaction effects cost SEK 51m of operating profit in 2025 and about 5 points of Trade's organic EBITA in Q1 2026, and management expects a tailwind from Q2 2026 as a stronger krona lowers euro and dollar purchase costs. (6) Industrial upturn: order books in automation and electrification are rising; here price is value-based in project quotes and the margin expands with utilisation, which is the only condition in which Storskogen shows anything resembling real pricing, and it is confined to perhaps SEK 5bn of Industrial Technology and Automation sales. (7) Fixed-price contracts: the Services revenue model is over-time recognition on fixed prices; two subsidiaries lost heavily in 2022 on rigid fixed-price structures; the exposure is still undisclosed.

How this compares with peers, and what it says about the magnitude. Lifco mandates post-acquisition price rises of 3% to 7% and its dental software units raise prices annually; Addtech passes cost through "rapidly" because its components are critical and low-cost to the customer; Indutrade prices on value, not cost-plus; Indus's BETEK turned a tungsten spike into a 7.9-point margin gain. Storskogen shows none of these behaviours at group level; DNB Carnegie's own phrase is "weak pricing power". Storskogen resembles Volati (price competition in construction-exposed platforms, discounting in Tornum) and Sdiptech's slower units (six-to-nine-month lags, multi-year framework agreements). The honest placement is the bottom third of the cohort on pricing, which is consistent with the first set's competitive-advantage verdict (no moat, price-taker in construction). The model consequence is that Storskogen's organic growth is worth less margin than the same organic growth at Lifco or Addtech: at those companies 1% of organic growth carries 30% to 40% drop-through because much of it is price on a fixed cost base; at Storskogen 1% of organic growth is partly price that only recovers cost, so the drop-through on the price part is near zero and only the volume part leverages.

How price enters my base case, line by line. Revenue: organic growth 3.5% in 2026 and 2027 and 3% thereafter, of which price about 2% in 2026 to 2027 (wage and material inflation still running at 2% to 3% in Sweden and Germany, steel safeguards from July 2026 and tariffs adding to Industry input costs) and about 1.5% from 2028; volume 1.5% in 2026 and 2027 rising to 1.5% to 2% as Services and Product Solutions recover. By segment: Industry price 2% to 2.5%, volume 2.5% to 3% (fading in the second half of 2027 as the short-cycle restock plateaus); Services price 1% (under pressure in Infrastructure, better in Business Services), volume 1% to 2%; Trade price 1% to 2%, volume 0% to 1% (consumer-facing, with the professional channel better). Gross margin: Industry stable at the Q2 2026 level; Trade up 50 to 100 basis points in the second half of 2026 and first half of 2027 as foreign-currency inventory bought at older, weaker-krona rates clears and new purchases come in cheaper (EUR/SEK toward 10.50 on Nordea's path), then flat; Services down 30 to 50 basis points in 2026 (price pressure plus wage inflation on fixed-price work), recovering 2027 to 2028 as volume returns and contracts roll. Adjusted EBITA margin contribution from price: about +10 basis points in 2026 (Trade currency net of Services pressure), +10 in 2027 (Trade), zero in 2028 to 2030; the Trade currency effect is a two-year windfall, and a reversal in the krona would turn it into a two-year drag of the same size, which is the reason I do not let it build into the long-run margin.

Predictability and skew. Predictability is moderate for Industry (a demonstrated one-quarter lag), low for Trade (the currency is unhedged and the lag is long) and low for Services (contract terms undisclosed). The skew for the next twelve months is slightly positive on the margin line (the krona tailwind is in motion and visible; Industry pricing is demonstrated) and neutral on revenue. Over two to four years the skew is negative on real pricing power: a renewed inflation shock from tariffs or steel would cost Services margin before it was recovered, while a disinflationary period would reduce the price component of organic growth to 1% and expose how little volume growth the portfolio generates. The tests for the Consumer: Industry margin holding above 11% with materials still rising (pass-through intact); Trade margin above 9.5% in the first half of 2027 (currency windfall delivered); Services margin back above 10% for two consecutive quarters (price pressure easing); and the share of organic growth that management attributes to price in its quarterly commentary, which is the one disclosure that would let the model separate price from volume properly and which the company does not yet give.

P-05

Reinforcing or balancing feedback loops: how they shape the main line items over the next 18 months to four years

C56

Question asked After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: three loops are at work and one of them dominates the model. The reinforcing acquisition loop (free cash flow buys EBITA at 7x, which raises EBITDA, which widens leverage headroom, which funds more deals, each unpriced by consensus until completion) has restarted and, on my base case, can run for the whole four-year horizon without reaching the 2.5x ceiling, because at SEK 1.6bn to SEK 2.3bn of annual spend the engine is cash-funded: it adds about 4% to 5.5% a year to revenue and 5% to 7% a year to EBITA from 2027, which is more than half of the EBITA growth in my base case. The balancing loops on the acquisition side (amortisation rising SEK 56m per SEK 1bn deployed, minority put liabilities growing with subsidiary performance, goodwill at 78% of purchase price, no internal audit as the unit count rises again) do not stop the loop within the horizon but they cap what reaches reported EPS and they raise the probability of a late, large surprise (an impairment or a control failure) rather than of a gradual deterioration. The cyclical loop is the swing factor for 2027: in Industry, order intake and utilisation reinforce each other upward now; in Services, construction weakness, fixed personnel cost and price competition reinforce each other downward now, and whichever of these two turns first sets the 2027 margin. Net effect on the line items: revenue growth stepping up from 2% to 6% to 8% as the acquisition loop gathers pace; EBITA margin rising 60 to 80 basis points over three years, of which the acquisition mix is about a third; adjusted EPS compounding at 12% to 14% a year; reported EPS compounding two to three points slower as amortisation rises; and free cash flow growing more slowly than EBITDA because growth consumes working capital and the put liabilities consume cash.

The positive loop, quantified, step by step. Step 1, cash: operating cash flow of SEK 2.45bn in 2025 (74% adjusted cash conversion), SEK 653m in the first half of 2026 after a seasonal and receivables build; I model free cash flow after interest, tax, capital spending and leases of SEK 1.6bn in 2026, SEK 1.7bn in 2027, SEK 1.9bn in 2028, SEK 2.0bn in 2029 and SEK 2.4bn in 2030. Step 2, claims on that cash: dividends at 15% of reported profit (SEK 0.19bn rising to SEK 0.3bn), minority buyouts and earn-outs (SEK 759m in 2025 including deals; management says "substantially smaller" in 2027; I carry SEK 200m to SEK 300m a year inside the acquisitions line). Step 3, deployable cash: SEK 1.2bn to SEK 2bn a year, plus leverage headroom that grows by about SEK 0.4bn for every SEK 0.4bn of EBITDA added at a constant 2.5x. Step 4, what it buys: at 7x EBITA on a 10% to 12% margin, SEK 1bn buys about SEK 140m of EBITA and SEK 1.2bn to SEK 1.4bn of sales (4% of 2025 sales); the 2026 vintage (Darlington SEK 99m, Safi SEK 106m, Verdant SEK 126m, Duratec SEK 112m, Hawedo SEK 93m, plus add-ons, about SEK 650m of annual sales for perhaps SEK 500m to SEK 600m of enterprise value) is the proof of price discipline but at a scale that adds under 2% to sales. Step 5, consensus: DNB Carnegie "does not normally reflect" uncompleted acquisitions, and its leverage path (2.7x in 2025 to 1.7x by 2028) shows that its model deploys little cash after 2026 even while it assumes 5% sales from M&A, an inconsistency the Consumer should note: whichever way DNB Carnegie resolves it, each completed deal is an estimate upgrade. Step 6, the loop closes: my cadence of SEK 1.6bn (2027), SEK 1.9bn (2028), SEK 2.1bn (2029) and SEK 2.3bn (2030) adds about SEK 230m, SEK 270m, SEK 300m and SEK 330m of annualised EBITA, 7% to 8% of the base each year, while net debt rises only SEK 0.6bn to SEK 0.8bn a year because most of the spend is funded from cash flow, so leverage falls to about 2.1x. The loop is self-funding at this cadence; it would hit 2.5x by about 2028 only at SEK 3bn a year of spend, which is the ceiling cadence and roughly the Lifco or Indutrade pace scaled to Storskogen's size.

How long such loops have run, and what stopped them. Lifco: a decade at 6x to 8x and leverage of 1x to 2x, 12 points a year of EBITA growth from deals; Indutrade and Addtech through the 2023 to 2025 downturn without pausing; Indus with a stated EUR 500m programme to 2030 at 7x to 7.5x. Storskogen in 2021 to 2022 ran the loop debt-funded at up to 10x and 4.35x leverage and the rate rise stopped it; Volati's post-Salix leverage (2.9x reported, higher pro forma) has stopped its loop in 2026. Sdiptech is the recovery precedent: leverage 3.31x to 2.81x via ten divestments, then a restart with SEK 2.4bn of available funds. The lesson that applies to Storskogen is that the loop stops when leverage meets a rate shock, not when it meets a growth slowdown; at 2.1x to 2.4x with maturities to 2028 to 2030, a rate shock in 2027 would slow the cadence rather than halt it, which is a different and better situation than in 2022. The constraint over the horizon is organisational (an M&A head doubling as Services head, a central team the sector research calls short of investment managers, deal "inflow volatility" noted in Q1 2026) and competitive (the expert view that Nordic multiples have moved to 8x to 10x EBITDA, against the company's 7x EBITA cap, which steers it toward smaller or non-Nordic targets), not financial.

The negative loop, and where it bites. Amortisation of acquired intangibles: SEK 175m in Q2 2026, about SEK 690m for 2026, rising with each deal by about SEK 56m per SEK 1bn (the annual report's own ratio: SEK 552m of 2025 acquisitions added SEK 31m); offset by run-off of the 2020 to 2022 vintage's customer relationships (2 to 15 years), so I model SEK 740m in 2027, SEK 810m in 2028, SEK 880m in 2029 and SEK 950m in 2030. This is non-cash and excluded from the brokers' EPS, but it is the accounting cost of the loop and it keeps reported EPS at about two-thirds of adjusted EPS throughout. Minority put liabilities: SEK 1,740m at mid-2026 (up SEK 267m in six months, SEK 205m of it remeasurement), valued on performance multiples, exercisable three to five years after each deal; the better the acquired units do, the larger the claim. I carry SEK 200m to SEK 300m a year of settlements; the risk is a cluster of exercises in one year (the 2021 to 2022 vintage reaches its windows in 2025 to 2027). Goodwill and control: 78% of 2026 purchase prices went to goodwill; the 2024 impairment of SEK 920m was 10% of then-goodwill; there is no internal audit across the units as the count rises from 113. The AlphaSense work finds no evidence on whether the 2020 to 2022 vintage's organic earnings held after purchase (Storskogen does not disclose it; Teqnion does), and group organic EBITA of -4% in the first half of 2026 is the only proxy. For the model: a recurring SEK 60m of items affecting comparability, and a cone that includes one SEK 0.5bn to SEK 1.5bn non-cash impairment somewhere in 2027 to 2029 at perhaps one-in-three probability, which would not touch adjusted EPS or cash but would move the equity ratio and sentiment.

The cyclical loop, and the 2027 question. Industry: automation and electrification order books rising, reshoring, a strong June finish, 6% organic growth and a 21% incremental margin in Q2 2026; the reinforcing part is that utilisation raises margin, which funds growth investment (capital spending at 2.3% of sales in Q2, the J&D Pierce coating facility), which wins larger projects. The balancing part: project timing (a strong June can borrow from Q3), Product Solutions' under-utilised factories, and the sector's expectation of a restock plateau by Q4 2026. I model Industry organic growth of 5% in the second half of 2026 fading to 3% by the second half of 2027. Services: construction weakness, personnel at 46% of costs and price competition reinforce each other downward, as Q1 2026 showed (EBITA -36% on flat sales); the reverse loop (volume returns, utilisation rises, price pressure eases) has not started, Swedish housing starts are at 8,000 and the Riksbank is about to hike. I model Services at a 9.0% margin in 2026, 9.6% in 2027 and 10.2% in 2028, which assumes the loop turns in mid-2027, a year later than management's tone implies. Trade: the retailer destocking loop (retailers cut inventory, Trade's volumes fall, Trade's units cut cost) is balancing and short; a restock would flip it within two quarters; the krona loop (stronger krona, lower import cost, higher margin, with a lag) is running in Trade's favour now.

The interplay the Consumer should hold. The three loops are connected through one variable, organic EBITA growth: positive organic EBITA raises EBITDA, which widens the acquisition headroom and lowers the amortisation-to-EBITA ratio, and it is what the market will read as evidence that the engine is sound (the sector digest's "organic growth is its evidence"); negative organic EBITA (as in the first half of 2026) means the acquisition loop is running to stand still, which is the Volati and 2023 Storskogen pattern. My base case has organic EBITA growth of about +3% in the second half of 2026, +5% in 2027 and +4% in 2028, so the acquisition loop adds to a growing base; the bear case has organic EBITA flat, in which case the deals still add 5% to 7% a year but the market will pay less for each of them.

P-06

Track record of delivery: how much weight the forecasts should carry, and in which direction they should be leaned

❌ D46

Question asked After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

Judgement: the forecasts should carry moderate weight on the lines management controls and low weight on the lines the cycle controls, and they should be leaned as follows: below management's targets on every count (the 15% EBITA growth target for 2025 to 2027 is unreachable and the 10% margin arrives in 2029 in my base case, not 2027); in line with DNB Carnegie on 2026 and 2027 EBITA and EPS (SEK 1.11 and SEK 1.25 against my SEK 1.12 and SEK 1.27) and above it from 2028 as the acquisition cadence compounds (my SEK 1.44 against its SEK 1.36); and with Nordea's EPS set aside as being on a different basis. On the quarterly pattern, lean the seasonally weak quarters (Q1, Q3) below the run-rate and the strong quarters (Q2, Q4) slightly above, because that is where the company has beaten and missed. The record justifies this lean: since listing the company has missed every profit target it set and delivered every balance-sheet action it promised, beaten consensus by 2% to 8% in Q2 to Q4 and missed it in Q1, and estimate revisions have run negative for a year and stopped only in the last month. The one direction the record does not justify is the sell-side's: both brokers hold Buy with targets 25% to 45% above the price after a year of cutting estimates, which is a divergence the dashboard rates D, and it means the risk to the forecasts over the next two quarters is more from the Q3 and Q1 prints than from the outer years.

The record, sorted by who controls the line. Controlled by management, and delivered: net debt down SEK 4bn to SEK 4.6bn in three years to 2.3x to 2.4x; cash conversion above 70% every year (104%, 97%, 74%, 74% rolling); interest cost down by a quarter in 2025 and halving again in the first half of 2026; eleven divestments in 2023, nine in 2024, two in 2025 to 2026; parent costs halved; maturities pushed to 2028 to 2030; acquisitions restarted at 7x or below, two platforms in Q2 2026 ahead of broker expectations. Controlled by the cycle, and missed: adjusted EBITA growth of 1%, 0% and -3% in 2023 to 2025 against 15%; margin 9.0%, 9.4%, 9.4% against above 10%; organic growth below GDP plus 1 to 2 points in each year since 2022; Services profitability. Against consensus: Q3 2024 beat 8%, Q4 2024 beat, Q3 2025 beat 3% (SEK 759m against SEK 737m), Q4 2025 in line with FactSet and 2% above Infront, Q1 2026 miss 3% to 6%, Q2 2026 beat 2%. Estimate path: FY2026 EPS consensus down 18.5% over twelve months, FY2027 down 13% (EBITDA -4%, sales +3%, so the cuts are margin and below-the-line), flat over one month; target price down 11% over three months (DNB Carnegie SEK 18.0 to SEK 15.0 in four steps, the last on 20 October 2026 for lower peer multiples and a more cautious Services view); buy share up 20 points to 80%.

What the record implies for each forecast line. Revenue: moderately reliable; the company's organic prints have been small and well explained, and divestments and currency, not demand, explain most of the misses; lean 1 point below the brokers in 2026 for currency (my +2.0% against +3.4%) and above from 2027 for acquisitions. Margin: the least reliable line; the company has guided to "above 10%" for five years and printed 9.0% to 9.4%; lean the margin below any management framing and roughly in line with DNB Carnegie (9.2% in 2026, 9.7% in 2027), with the Services recovery later than management implies; weight 30% to 40% on reaching 10% in 2027, 60% on 2029. Net financial items: highly reliable; the actions are executed and disclosed; lean at the run-rate (SEK 580m) rather than at DNB Carnegie's 6% assumption. Tax: reliable within a 24% to 26% band; the interest-deduction structure is a known risk to 27% to 28%. Cash flow: reliable on the annual numbers, unreliable quarter to quarter (48% conversion in Q2 2026 on a receivables build); lean free cash flow below the FactSet consensus for 2027 to 2028 (my SEK 1.7bn and SEK 1.9bn after leases against SEK 2.1bn and SEK 2.4bn) because the brokers appear to assume working-capital release that growth will not allow. Acquisitions: the record since the restart is nine months of deals at the stated discipline but a tenth of the size needed; weight management's "step up quarter by quarter" at 60%, and treat each quarter without a rising deal count as evidence against the base case.

Why the lean on the targets is firm and the lean on consensus is mild. The 15% target requires 2027 EBITA of about SEK 4.7bn against my SEK 3.4bn; even a SEK 3bn acquisition year in 2027 would deliver about SEK 3.7bn to SEK 3.8bn. Management has conditioned the target ("especially if the economic environment improves") rather than withdrawn it, and the 2026 Share Savings Programme's 15% hurdle restates it; the first checkpoint, the full-year 2027 report in February 2028, will be a visible miss, and the Consumer should expect new targets or a capital markets day in 2027 to 2028 as the narrative response. Consensus, by contrast, has already discounted the target (EBITDA +0.7% in 2026, +5.4% in 2027) and has been cut for a year, so the remaining risk in its 2026 to 2027 numbers is mostly the Services margin and the Q3 and Q1 seasonality, each worth 2% to 4% of EBITA; its 2028 number omits the acquisition cadence and is the one most likely to rise.

How much weight, in one line each. Management targets: 20% (use as the upper bound of the cone). DNB Carnegie 2026 to 2027: 60% (use as the base for EBITA, correct net financial items down and the Q3 margin down). DNB Carnegie 2028: 50% (lean up for acquisitions, down for the Services margin). Nordea: use the EBITDA and revenue lines only. My base case: 55% on the central path, with the width given in P-09.

P-08

Below-operating-profit items and share count: how they shape EPS, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years

C58

Question asked After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

Judgement: the lines below adjusted EBITA turn from the dominant drag of 2022 to 2025 into a neutral-to-mildly-positive bridge for 2026 and then into a steady two-to-three-point-a-year drag on reported (not adjusted) EPS growth from 2027. Over 2022 to 2025, net interest of SEK 624m to SEK 1,044m, amortisation of about SEK 700m to SEK 800m, a SEK 920m impairment, a 76.5% tax year and recurring divestment losses took reported EPS from SEK 0.87 in 2022 to SEK 0.47, -SEK 0.03 and SEK 0.63; adjusted EBITA barely moved over the same period. Over 2026 to 2030 the interest line is flat after its 2026 step-down (SEK 580m to SEK 650m), tax sits at 25% to 25.5% with a known risk to 27% to 28%, amortisation rises from SEK 690m to about SEK 950m with the deal cadence, items affecting comparability run at about SEK 60m a year, minority interests in the income statement stay negligible while put liabilities absorb SEK 200m to SEK 300m of cash a year, the share count is flat (no further buybacks in the base case, cash-settled incentives, a small hurdle-share conversion possible in 2029), and the dividend follows 15% of reported profit. The result is adjusted EPS of SEK 1.12, 1.27, 1.44, 1.58, 1.73 (compounding about 12% a year from 2026) against reported EPS of SEK 0.76, 0.91, 1.05, 1.16, 1.28 (compounding about 14% from a lower base but structurally at two-thirds of adjusted), and free cash flow after leases of SEK 1.6bn rising to SEK 2.4bn, which grows more slowly than EBITDA because working capital and leases absorb part of the growth.

Net financial items. Covered in P-02: SEK 580m (2026), SEK 575m (2027), SEK 590m (2028), SEK 620m (2029), SEK 650m (2030), against SEK 828m in 2025 and SEK 1,044m in 2024. The 2025-to-2026 fall is worth SEK 0.11 of adjusted EPS and is the single largest item in 2026's EPS growth; nothing of comparable size is left in this line. Risks: STIBOR (SEK 69m per 100 basis points, about SEK 0.03 of EPS), FX in financial items (SEK 42m in 2025), the late-2027 Bond 1 refinancing (upside of SEK 10m to SEK 25m a year on a lower spread or a rating upgrade; downside of SEK 40m in a stressed market).

Amortisation of acquired intangibles. SEK 690m in 2026 (SEK 168m and SEK 175m in the first two quarters; DNB Carnegie SEK 683m), rising with deployment at about SEK 56m per SEK 1bn and partly offset by run-off, to SEK 740m, SEK 810m, SEK 880m, SEK 950m. It is excluded from DNB Carnegie's EPS and from mine; it is included in Nordea's and in the company's own adjusted EPS. The Consumer should present valuation on both: on my numbers the shares at SEK 11 trade at 9.8x 2026 adjusted EPS and 14.5x reported, 8.7x and 12.1x on 2027. The gap narrows only if the deal cadence stops, which is the wrong reason to want it narrower.

Tax. Reported 24.4% in 2025 after an internal restructuring that improved interest deductibility under Swedish interest-limitation rules, 28.5% in 2023, 76.5% in 2024 (non-deductible divestment losses). Peers sit at 22.8% to 23.8%, so there is little further room. DNB Carnegie models 25.5% for 2026 and 24.0% for 2027; I use 25.5% for 2026 (the Perfect Hair loss is non-deductible) and 25% thereafter, with the stated risk that a change in Swedish interest-limitation rules or a further divestment loss pushes the rate to 27% to 28% (worth SEK 0.03 to SEK 0.05 of EPS). Cash tax has run below book tax (paid tax down 24% in 2025); I do not assume that persists.

One-offs, divestment charges and impairments. Items affecting comparability have appeared in most years: SEK 920m impairment (2024), SEK 7m loss on Motavo (Q4 2025), SEK 65m loss on Perfect Hair (Q1 2026), SEK 28m in Q2 2026 (earn-out remeasurement SEK 11m, central restructuring SEK 7m, other), SEK 38m of hedge losses in Q1 2026, SEK 22m incentive-provision reversals that flattered two earlier quarters. I carry SEK 125m in 2026 and SEK 60m a year thereafter below adjusted EBITA, and a one-in-three probability of a SEK 0.5bn to SEK 1.5bn non-cash impairment in 2027 to 2029 outside the base case. These items are why reported net income has been so much more volatile than adjusted EBITA (reported net income SEK 1,435m, SEK 778m, -SEK 52m, SEK 1,063m over 2022 to 2025) and why the Consumer should put less weight on reported EPS growth rates and more on the level.

Minorities, put options and earn-outs. Income-statement minorities are negligible (SEK 1m in FactSet's capital structure) because the company consolidates put-option subsidiaries as if wholly owned and books the put liability instead of equity. The liability was SEK 1,740m at June 2026 (SEK 1,473m at end-2025), with SEK 205m of first-half remeasurement; contingent considerations SEK 80m. Cash settlements were a large part of the SEK 759m spent on combinations and buyouts in 2025 and management says they will be "substantially smaller" in 2027. I carry SEK 250m to SEK 300m in 2026 and SEK 200m to SEK 250m a year thereafter inside the acquisitions line. Two points for the Consumer: where the remeasurement is booked (if through financial items it would explain part of the gap between adjusted and reported profit and is a volatility source; the disclosure read for this set does not settle it), and that a cluster of exercises from the 2021 to 2022 vintage could lift one year's settlements to SEK 500m, which would displace one platform deal rather than threaten anything.

Share count, buybacks and dividend. Shares outstanding about 1,678m after the SEK 100m buyback of 8,865,000 B shares completed in January 2026 (1,695m issued including 8.6m C1 hurdle shares that convert only if price hurdles are met by June 2029; the share savings programme is cash-settled through a swap). The board holds an authorisation of up to about SEK 1,337m but management's stated priority is acquisitions and the 15% payout; my base case has no further buybacks, which I regard as a capital-allocation error at 9x to 10x adjusted earnings (the company's own deals at 7x EBITA imply its shares at about 9x EV/EBITA are not obviously cheaper than targets, but a buyback carries no integration risk and no amortisation). Change in the share count other than through buybacks: zero in 2026 to 2028; a possible 4m to 9m in 2029 from the C1 conversion if the share price doubles, which I leave at zero. Dividend: 15% of reported profit, SEK 0.11 for 2025; my base case SEK 0.12, 0.14, 0.16, 0.18, 0.20 for 2026 to 2030 (consensus SEK 0.13, 0.145, 0.16), a cash cost of SEK 0.2bn to SEK 0.33bn a year, 1.1% to 1.8% yield at SEK 11.

Free cash flow. Definition used throughout: operating cash flow after interest and tax, less capital spending including intangibles, less lease payments, before acquisitions, minority buyouts and dividends. 2025 on this definition was about SEK 1.3bn (SEK 2,451m operating cash flow less SEK 658m of capital spending less about SEK 480m of lease payments); FactSet's SEK 1,732m excludes leases and intangibles. My path: SEK 1.58bn (2026: operating cash flow about SEK 2.75bn after a weak first half of SEK 653m, capital spending SEK 700m at 2.1% of sales, leases SEK 480m), SEK 1.72bn (2027), SEK 1.85bn (2028), SEK 2.04bn (2029), SEK 2.36bn (2030). Against EBITDA growth of 42% over the period, free cash flow grows 49% from a depressed 2026 base but is held back by a working-capital build of SEK 350m to SEK 600m a year at about 22% to 23% of sales (FactSet's year-end definition; the company's narrower measure is about 15%), by lease payments rising with the unit count, and by capital spending at 2.1% of sales (above Lifco's 0.8% and Addtech's 0.8%, in line with Indutrade's 1.8%, below Sdiptech's 3% ceiling). Cash conversion on the company's measure should hold at 70% to 80%; the 97% to 104% of 2023 to 2024 was working-capital liquidation and will not recur.

Versus the last four to six years, in one comparison. 2022 to 2025: adjusted EBITA roughly flat at SEK 3.1bn to SEK 3.2bn; net financial items SEK 0.6bn to SEK 1.0bn; reported net income swinging from SEK 1.4bn to a loss; free cash flow flattered by working capital; share count flat; dividends SEK 0.07 to SEK 0.11. 2026 to 2030: adjusted EBITA growing 8% to 10% a year; net financial items SEK 0.58bn to SEK 0.65bn; reported net income SEK 1.3bn rising to SEK 2.1bn; free cash flow growing with EBITDA but burdened by growth; share count flat; dividends doubling. The below-the-line items stop being the story; the story moves to EBITA, which is where it should be.

P-09

The net likely impact on the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years

C55

Question asked After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

Judgement: taken together, the items in this set turn Storskogen from a company whose adjusted EBITA was flat for three years and whose EPS was set by its finance costs into one whose adjusted EBITA grows 8% to 10% a year and whose EPS grows 12% a year on the adjusted basis, with the growth coming about 55% from acquisitions, 25% from organic volume and mix, 15% from the 2026 interest step-down and 5% from the margin levers (pricing, cost, currency); the margin reaches 10% in 2029, two years after management's target period; free cash flow grows but more slowly than EBITDA; and leverage falls to about 2.1x, which leaves room the base case does not use. Against the last four to six years the change is large on growth and small on quality: returns on capital rise from about 10% to about 12%, reported EPS stays at two-thirds of adjusted, and the balance sheet carries more goodwill, more put liabilities and more amortisation than before. The cone around this path is wide over twelve months (adjusted EBITA plus or minus 8% to 10% on the two cyclical pockets and the Q3 and Q1 prints) and wider over four years, with the width set by the deal cadence (zero to SEK 3bn a year) more than by the cycle.

The line items, last four to six years against the next four, on the brokers' basis where consensus exists.

Revenue: 2020 to 2025 grew +45%, +96%, +96%, +5%, -5%, -3% (acquisition-driven to 2022, then divestment- and currency-driven shrinkage). Next four: +2.0% (2026), +6.3%, +8.0%, +8.0%, +7.5%, reaching about SEK 45bn by 2030; organic 3% to 3.5%, acquired 1.5% rising to 5.5%, currency -1.3% in 2026 and -1% in 2027 then neutral, divestments -1.7% in 2026. Consensus: +3.4%, +5.3%, +3.6%; I am below in 2026 on currency and above from 2027 on acquisitions, which consensus does not model.

Adjusted EBITDA margin (brokers' basis, after IFRS 16): 11.9% (2023), 12.8% (2024), 12.5% (2025). Next four: 12.4%, 12.6%, 12.9%, 13.0%, 13.1%. Consensus 12.2%, 12.2%, 12.4%; I am slightly above in 2026 on the second half and above from 2027 on Services recovery and acquired mix. Adjusted EBITA margin: 9.0%, 9.4%, 9.4% becoming 9.3%, 9.6%, 9.9%, 10.0%, 10.1%; adjusted EBITA SEK 3.13bn, 3.43bn, 3.83bn, 4.18bn, 4.54bn.

Depreciation (excluding acquired intangibles, including right-of-use assets): SEK 1.03bn in 2025, 3.1% of sales; SEK 1.05bn, 1.09bn, 1.18bn, 1.27bn, 1.36bn, held at 3.0% to 3.1% as acquired units bring assets.

Net financial items: SEK 1,005m, 1,044m, 828m (2023 to 2025) becoming SEK 580m, 575m, 590m, 620m, 650m.

Tax: 28.5%, 76.5%, 24.4% becoming 25.5% then 25%.

Adjusted EPS (DNB Carnegie's basis): SEK 0.77, 0.87, 0.95 (2023 to 2025) becoming SEK 1.12, 1.27, 1.44, 1.58, 1.73. DNB Carnegie SEK 1.11, 1.25, 1.36 (2026 to 2028). Reported EPS: SEK 0.47, -0.03, 0.63 becoming SEK 0.76, 0.91, 1.05, 1.16, 1.28.

Free cash flow after leases: about SEK 2.0bn, 1.8bn, 1.3bn (2023 to 2025, the first two flattered by working-capital release) becoming SEK 1.58bn, 1.72bn, 1.85bn, 2.04bn, 2.36bn. Capital spending including intangibles SEK 0.7bn to SEK 0.9bn (2.0% to 2.1% of sales); lease payments SEK 0.48bn rising to SEK 0.61bn; working capital (FactSet year-end definition) 23.4% of sales at end-2025 easing to 22% by 2030.

Acquisitions less disposals, including minority buyouts and earn-outs: SEK 0.5bn, 0.9bn (2024 to 2025) becoming SEK 0.65bn, 1.85bn, 2.1bn, 2.3bn, 2.5bn. Buybacks: SEK 85m (2025), SEK 10m (2026), nil thereafter. Dividends SEK 0.19bn rising to SEK 0.33bn.

Net debt (company basis, including leases): SEK 9.5bn at end-2025 to about SEK 9.1bn, 9.7bn, 10.5bn, 11.4bn, 12.2bn; leverage 2.3x, 2.2x, 2.15x, 2.1x, 2.1x, 2.05x.

Where the net impact is concentrated. Three lines carry the change: revenue growth (from negative to high single digits, mostly bought), the EBITA margin (60 to 80 basis points over three years from mix, Services recovery and Trade currency, with the cost and pricing levers each worth about 10 to 20 basis points) and net financial items (one step down in 2026). Everything else is steady or mechanical. This matters for the predictability work: the forecast's difference from history rests on two drivers, the deal cadence and the Services margin, and on one banked item, which is a concentrated rather than diffuse set of drivers. The deal cadence is observable quarterly and controlled by management; the Services margin is observable quarterly and controlled by the Swedish construction cycle and by whoever runs the segment; the interest line is already visible in the half-year accounts.

The cone, so the Consumer can test it. Twelve months (to the Q3 2027 report): adjusted EBITA 2026 between SEK 3.0bn and SEK 3.2bn (a Q3 miss on Trade restraint and Services pricing, or a Q4 beat on Industry and the krona); 2027 between SEK 3.1bn (construction leg down, deals at the 2026 pace, Services at 8.5%) and SEK 3.7bn (Services back to 10.5% by mid-2027, SEK 2.5bn of deals in 2027, Industry holding 5% organic), a range of -10% to +8% around SEK 3.43bn, with the skew slightly negative over the next two prints (Q3 and Q1 are the weak quarters and the Riksbank hikes in between) and slightly positive thereafter. Four years: 2030 adjusted EBITA between SEK 3.6bn (no deal step-up, margin stuck at 9.5%) and SEK 5.5bn (SEK 3bn a year of deals, 10.5% margin), around SEK 4.5bn; adjusted EPS between SEK 1.30 and SEK 2.20 around SEK 1.73. The width is set by the engine: at zero deals the company compounds EPS at 5% to 6% a year from organic growth and the interest base; at the ceiling cadence it compounds at 18% to 20%.

Versus the sector and peers. Lifco compounds EBITA at 15% to 20% at a 22% margin; Indutrade and Addtech at 10% to 15% at 13% to 16%; Sdiptech is restarting at a 21% core margin with 11% organic growth; Indus is growing on a commodity windfall; Volati is shrinking. Storskogen's 8% to 10% EBITA growth at a 9% to 10% margin is the lowest-quality version of the compounder pattern in the cohort, and the market's 9x to 10x EV/EBITA against 20x-plus for the leaders reflects that. What the next four years change is the direction (growth returns) and the funding (cash, not debt); what they do not change is the margin and return gap, which is why the forecasts support a modest re-rating toward 11x to 12x if delivered and not a convergence with peers.

P-10

The base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years

C56

Question asked Pulling together all the above answers, in all sets so far, what is your carefully considered base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: my base case is a cash-funded restart of the acquisition engine at a cadence that rises from about SEK 0.65bn of total deal and buyout spending in 2026 to SEK 2.5bn in 2030, laid over an organic business that grows 3% to 3.5% a year with Industry leading, Services recovering from mid-2027 and Trade flat, and over a finance line that has already taken its step down. It produces revenue of SEK 33.8bn (2026), 35.9bn, 38.8bn, 41.9bn, 45.0bn; adjusted EBITA of SEK 3.13bn (9.3%), 3.43bn (9.6%), 3.83bn (9.9%), 4.18bn (10.0%), 4.54bn (10.1%); adjusted EBITDA margins of 12.4%, 12.6%, 12.9%, 13.0%, 13.1%; adjusted EPS of SEK 1.12, 1.27, 1.44, 1.58, 1.73 and reported EPS of SEK 0.76, 0.91, 1.05, 1.16, 1.28; free cash flow after leases of SEK 1.58bn, 1.72bn, 1.85bn, 2.04bn, 2.36bn; dividends of SEK 0.12 to SEK 0.20; leverage falling from 2.3x to about 2.05x. It sits in line with DNB Carnegie through 2027 and above it in 2028, below the company's targets throughout, and it carries a slightly negative skew over the next two quarterly prints and a slightly positive skew over two to four years because the unused leverage headroom is a real option that the base case does not exercise.

The operational drivers, by segment, with what each must do.

Industry (43% of sales; 2025 sales SEK 14,319m, margin about 10.6% on my estimate of the undisclosed segment split, 11.0% in Q2 2026). Organic growth 5% in the second half of 2026 (order books, automation and electrification, international markets ahead of Sweden), 4% in the first half of 2027, 3% thereafter as the short-cycle restock plateaus; Product Solutions flat until 2027 then recovering 2% to 3% a year. Acquired growth 1% in 2026 (Darlington), 3% to 5% from 2027 (the UK pipeline is the strongest; automation and electronics platforms). Margin 10.9% in 2026, 11.2% in 2027, 11.4% in 2028, held there: price recovers cost with a one-quarter lag, utilisation in Product Solutions is the upside, project timing and tariffs the downside. Segment sales about SEK 14.8bn, 15.7bn, 17.0bn, 18.4bn, 19.8bn.

Trade (29%; SEK 9,588m, margin 8.7% in 2025, 9.2% in Q2 2026). Reported sales fall 3.6% in 2026 (Perfect Hair and Motavo out, SEK 560m; consumer destocking; krona translation) then grow 5% to 7% from 2027 on acquisitions (Verdant, Duratec, Hawedo, more B2B distribution) and 1% to 2% organic. Margin 8.9% in 2026 (a weak first quarter, then the currency windfall), 9.5% in 2027, 9.6% in 2028, then 9.5% as the currency effect normalises; the mix shift to professional B2B and proprietary brands is the structural support. Segment sales about SEK 9.2bn, 9.8bn, 10.5bn, 11.2bn, 11.9bn.

Services (28%; SEK 9,232m, margin about 10.0% in 2025 on my estimate, 7.7% in Q1 and 10.3% in Q2 2026). Organic growth 2% in 2026 (Infrastructure flat, Business Services up), 2.5% in 2027, 3% thereafter; acquired growth 1.5% in 2026 (Safi), 3% to 5% from 2027 (fire safety, digital and recurring services, non-Nordic). Margin 9.0% in 2026, 9.6% in 2027, 10.2% in 2028, 10.5% in 2029 to 2030: this assumes a permanent head by early 2027, construction volumes stabilising in the second half of 2027, selective bidding and standardisation adding 50 to 100 basis points over two years, and no further workforce cuts. This is the line on which the base case is most exposed; a construction leg down in 2027 would hold the margin at 8.5% to 9.0% and cost SEK 60m to SEK 100m against the base. Segment sales about SEK 9.6bn, 10.2bn, 11.0bn, 12.0bn, 13.0bn.

Central costs: SEK 170m to SEK 180m a year. Group EBITA as above.

The acquisition engine, dated. 2026: about SEK 650m of outflow, of which perhaps SEK 350m to SEK 400m for the six platforms and add-ons (SEK 650m of acquired annual sales) and the balance minority buyouts and earn-outs; acquired sales contribution to 2026 about 1.5%. 2027: SEK 1.6bn of new deals plus SEK 250m of buyouts; eight to twelve deals; acquired sales +4% (half-year effect plus the 2026 carry-over); acquired EBITA about SEK 230m annualised. 2028: SEK 1.9bn plus SEK 200m; acquired sales +5.5%. 2029 and 2030: SEK 2.1bn and SEK 2.3bn plus SEK 200m; acquired sales +5.5% a year. At 7x EBITA and 78% goodwill, each year adds about SEK 1.3bn to SEK 1.8bn of goodwill and SEK 90m to SEK 130m of annualised amortisation, net of run-off about SEK 60m to SEK 70m. Tests the Consumer should set: a rising deal count each quarter from Q4 2026; acquired sales contribution above 3% by Q2 2027 and above 5% by Q1 2028; multiples stated at or below 7x; leverage not rising above 2.3x in any quarter of 2027.

The quarterly phasing for the next 18 months (Part 3 of the forecast inputs). Q3 2026: revenue +1.8% (organic +3%, acquired +2%, divestments -1.7%, currency -1.5%) to about SEK 8.13bn, adjusted EBITA about SEK 770m (9.45%) against SEK 759m, with Trade's "customer restraint" and Services pricing the risks; EBITDA margin 12.5%. Q4 2026: +2.4% to about SEK 8.93bn, EBITA about SEK 860m (9.6%) against SEK 816m; EBITDA margin 12.7%. Q1 2027: +5.0% (acquisitions arriving, currency -2%), EBITA margin 8.4% against 8.1% (a normal winter assumed), EBITDA margin 11.5%. Q2 2027: +6.0% against a strong June comparison, EBITA 9.9%, EBITDA 13.0%. Q3 2027: +7.0%, EBITA 9.7%, EBITDA 12.7%. Q4 2027: +7.0%, EBITA 9.9%, EBITDA 13.0%. Full-year 2027: revenue SEK 35.9bn, EBITA SEK 3.43bn.

The finance and cash lines. Net financial items SEK 580m, 575m, 590m, 620m, 650m. Tax 25.5% then 25%. Amortisation of acquired intangibles SEK 690m, 740m, 810m, 880m, 950m. Items affecting comparability SEK 125m then SEK 60m. Shares 1,678m. Dividend SEK 0.12, 0.14, 0.16, 0.18, 0.20. Capital spending SEK 700m, 760m, 800m, 850m, 900m. Working capital 23.2%, 22.8%, 22.5%, 22.3%, 22.0% of sales at year-end (FactSet definition). Lease payments SEK 480m, 500m, 530m, 570m, 610m. Acquisitions less disposals SEK 650m, 1,850m, 2,100m, 2,300m, 2,500m. Buybacks SEK 10m then nil.

Where my base case sits against the other anchors. Against management: below on growth (EBITA +0.5%, +10%, +11%, +9%, +9% against a 15% average) and later on margin (10% in 2029 against 2027). Against DNB Carnegie: in line on 2026 to 2027 EBITA and EPS (within 2%), above on 2028 EPS by 6% (acquisitions), below on net financial items (run-rate against 6%), below on free cash flow (leases and working capital). Against Nordea: in line on revenue and within 1% to 3% on EBITDA; its EPS is on another basis. Against the first set's steer: consistent on direction; more explicit on the engine; later on Services; and firmer that leverage is not the constraint.

What would move me off the base case, with the direction. Up: two consecutive quarters of positive organic EBITA (the first could be Q3 2026), a Services head and a Services margin above 10% by Q2 2027, a deal count rising to three or four a quarter, a SEK 500m-plus platform, a rating upgrade before the late-2027 refinancing, or a buyback programme; any two of these would move 2028 adjusted EPS toward SEK 1.55 to SEK 1.60. Down: a Q3 2026 or Q1 2027 miss of 5% or more, a Services margin below 9% for a second consecutive full year, Industry organic growth falling below 2% as the restock plateaus, a Riksbank path of two or more hikes, a goodwill impairment or a control failure at a unit, or a quarter in which no deals close; any two would move 2027 adjusted EPS toward SEK 1.10 and restart the estimate cuts that stopped in October 2026.

Where I disagree with the first set, collected. (1) C-75: volume drop-through is 20% to 35% in the under-utilised units and about 20% at group level once labour adjusts, not "close to proportional"; the SEK 31m sensitivity is price-neutral. (2) C-49, C-67 and the Economic-strength group judgement: the 2027 consensus EPS growth of 21% is a basis artefact; on one basis it is 12.6%, and the interest step-down lands in 2026, not 2027. (3) C-73 and C-67: Nordea's EPS is not "stale" or "too low"; it is after amortisation of acquired intangibles, which also means the FactSet EPS median and mean are unusable and the consensus net income line (SEK 1,309m for 2026) is on the after-amortisation basis while the EPS median is not. (4) C-76 and the Risks group judgement: financial leverage is the largest amplifier in 2026 only; from 2027 it is one of the most predictable lines and the balance sheet has unused capacity, so the "window closes at the ceiling" pattern does not bind within four years at the stated cadence. (5) C-52: free cash flow for 2027 to 2028 should be modelled below the FactSet consensus (SEK 2.1bn and SEK 2.4bn), not at it, because growth consumes working capital and leases are a cash cost. (6) C-06 and C-80 on 2025 organic growth: DNB Carnegie's -3.2% is the reported figure; organic was about +1% (reported -3.2% plus about 2% of currency and about 2% of divestments); it does not change any forecast. (7) C-21 and C-18 on acquired growth of 2% to 4% in 2026: 1.5% is the arithmetic of the deals announced; 2027 to 2028 at 4% to 5.5% is consistent with the first set's range.
